#288. What Marco Rubio’s sidestepping of New Delhi says about the US’s stance on India

Rubio’s decision not to meet Narendra Modi last week was seen by some as a snub – but by focusing on business leaders and oil, the secretary of state made American priorities clear. Plus: the artist bringing a female perspective to men’s clothing

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

When the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, visited India for the second time this year, headlines concentrated on who he met and where he went.

The fact that Rubio bypassed New Delhi and did not meet the prime minister, Narendra Modi, during his trip last week, and instead traveled to the financial capital, Mumbai, where he met India’s foreign minister, Subrahmanyam Jaishankar (pictured top with Rubio), and business leaders, did not go unnoticed. Nor did his decision to travel to Jamnagar, the small Gujarati town that is home to Reliance Industries’ colossal oil refinery complex.

Bilateral ties between the two countries have come under strain under the second Trump administration, particularly over tariffs and India’s purchase of Russian oil. Yet, Rubio’s return to India just five months after his first visit as secretary of state signals that the country remains useful for president Donald Trump’s ambitions after all.

As director of the South Asia Program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, Milan Vaishnav has spent a decade tracking India’s place in the world, particularly its ties with the US. Vaishnav has described Trump 2.0 as “unbound” marking a new era in India-US relations.

Tracking foreign affairs, he told me, is a minefield right now. From the White House’s transactional approach to global alliances, ongoing conflicts in the Middle East and consequential elections around the world, politics is changing at a pace that means all predictions are off the table. “I think anyone who tells you they know exactly what is going on and how it fits into their neat theory of the world is lying to you,” says Vaishnav.

I asked him about the many confusing aspects of the US-India relationship, from the hostile political rhetoric to the US embrace of Pakistan, as well as Rubio’s latest visit.

Ahead of Rubio’s visit, several commentators noted the secretary of state’s “unusual” itinerary. Vaishnav thought the pre-visit chatter about Rubio skipping Delhi to visit Mumbai and Jamnagar was overblown. “Not every bilateral meeting has to take place in a national capital,” he says.

But that doesn’t mean it wasn’t noteworthy. “I do think the choice of venues was intentional. This administration and, particularly, this president, sees foreign policy through the lens of ‘deal-making’. And if you’re trying to drum up business deals, what better place to visit than Mumbai?” he says.

The Jamnagar visit, where Rubio met Mukesh Ambani, the chair of Reliance, also indicates some politicking. “At a time when petrol prices are extremely high in the United States and are likely to be a major issue in the 3 November midterm elections, the administration wants to show that it is doing everything it can to bring them down. India is a major consumer of oil, but it is also a major exporter of refined petroleum products,” says Vaishnav. “One of Rubio’s objectives was to encourage Reliance and, by extension, India to process more Venezuelan crude [oil],” he added.

While this could present an opportunity for India to advance relations, it’s not a given: the US president remains, after all – and to put it lightly – mercurial.

“The biggest plot twist for me has been the Trump administration’s embrace of Pakistan after the May 2025 conflict with India,” says Vaishnav. Pakistan’s standing in Washington had eroded over the years, especially under Joe Biden, who left office without ever having made a presidential phone call to his counterpart in Pakistan. “Many officials in Congress had grown exhausted with Pakistan’s double game, speaking the language of partnership on the one hand while undermining US interests on the other,” says Vaishnav.

But, after coming to power the second time, Trump thanked the country for its help in nabbing a suspect linked to a 2021 bombing in Afghanistan that killed 13 US service members. This reversal in Pakistan’s fortunes gathered pace after the May conflict with India, which Trump took credit for ending. In June, he welcomed Asim Munir, Pakistan’s army chief, to the White House, after Munir called for the president to be nominated for the Nobel peace prize.

“I chalk up the reversal to a few factors: Trump’s well-known strongman envy, Pakistan’s savvy diplomacy, and the prospect of energy and crypto deals involving members of Trump’s inner circle,” says Vaishnav. For him, it isyet another demonstration of how much weight Trump places on deals, transactions and short-term wins. “For New Delhi, this means Modi’s personal rapport with Trump and 25 years of investment in the bilateral relationship offer no guarantee against a 180-degree reversal in US policy,” he adds.

In a recent piece, Vaishnav’s colleague Sameer Lalwani pointed out that the US relationship with India remains more resilient than the political rhetoric may suggest with quiet, but nonetheless strategic, progress being made.

As Lalwani points out, bilateral trade reached a record high between India and the US in 2025; nearly $68bn worth of AI infrastructure investment in India has been pledged by US technology companies; and the two countries have carried out record number of joint military exercises this year.

Despite these wins, in terms of future bilateral relations, “there are at least three reasons for caution,” warns Vaishnav. First, he points to the onslaught of US actions that have dented Indian interests. Trump had imposed a punitive 50% tariff on India in 2025, which was reduced to 18% earlier this year, but without a comprehensive trade deal being reached. Second, on immigration, the White House’s H-1B visa and green card restrictions have disproportionately affected Indian workers in Silicon Valley.

“This has created a real trust deficit on the Indian side, and with good reason,” says Vaishnav. The frustration was on display earlier this month, when New Delhi branded remarks made by the US vice-president, JD Vance, about Indian professionals as “deeply offensive”. Furthermore, Vaishnav argues that while governments and private sector firms might be great at making announcements, they’re not always a guarantee of delivery.

His final caution is how political tensions can have a chilling effect. The trade negotiations offer a telling example: in January, the US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, blamed the lack of a trade deal on Modi’s failure to phone Trump, a characterisation that New Delhi has disputed. Vaishnav says: “Much of the progress we are seeing is the product of decisions made years ago. It is one thing to keep existing projects moving, but turbulence in the relationship could make new initiatives a heavier lift.”

If India is serious about making its ambition to be a global powerhouse a reality, Vaishnav thinks there’s a clearcut way to win. “I would argue that the most important investment is political – ensuring India remains a robust, inclusive, participatory democracy,” he says. “On the economic front, India has made massive new investments in physical and digital infrastructure. But the returns on those investments will be limited unless the government doubles down on reforms: more efficient dispute resolution, an impartial legal system, even-handed tax enforcementand regulatory certainty.”

Ultimately, neither side can afford to treat the other as expendable. The test now is whether strategic foundations can hold at a time of global political upheaval.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

That’s the number of trees Mumbai’s municipal authorities authorised to be felled over five months this year, to make way for development works, according to the Indian Express. Of these, about 3,500 will be permanently removed, while the rest will be replanted elsewhere.

Prarthna Singh has consistently placed the female identity at the heart of her artistic practice, documenting Indian women’s aspirations and acts of rebellion in male-dominated spaces. When commissioned to photograph rkivecity’s menswear line, made from discarded clothes reworked into new garments, she turned that same gaze outward. The setting was the acclaimed 151-year-old Mayo College boys’ school in Ajmer, Rajasthan – Singh attended the linked girls’ school, and credits it with shaping her rebellious spirit.

“I was thinking about how I could bring a sense of intimacy and camaraderie of boarding-school life to the images, because that exists,” she told me. One image shows a pair of hands holding another boy’s jacket, the faces left out of the frame, so the garment stands in for the familiarity they share. Seeing this work against the backdrop of students taking to the streets to fight for their rights lends the series a lasting significance. “This was just my way of showing all of this is connected. These worlds are not existing separately,” said Singh.

#284. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#283. Turkey's new deal on Mediterranean maritime boundaries with Libya is exacerbating issues with Greece and the EU

Turkey and Libya plan to redraw maritime boundaries in the Mediterranean as part of a new economic corridor. This has sparked strong protests from Greece, and has also drawn the European Union into the conflict.

On Tuesday, the parliament of Libya unanimously approved a maritime agreement with Turkey to establish a direct maritime economic corridor between the countries.

Turkey had agreed on the corridor with the UN-recognized Government of National Accord in western Libya in 2019. However, the agreement faced opposition from the rival Libyan government in the country's east. Now, though, the eastern parliament has reversed its previous opposition.

The decision, which was welcomed by Turkey's Foreign Ministry, is likely to contribute to tensions with other countries in the eastern Mediterranean — especially Greece .

The agreement establishes a direct maritime corridor between the southern coast of Turkey and the northern coast of Libya. Large oil and gas deposits have recently been discovered in the region in question — estimates suggest that natural gas reserves worth several hundred billion US dollars may lie beneath the seabed. Turkey and Libya aim to secure their economic rights to these raw materials and are seeking to jointly exploit the resources.

The planned corridor disregards Greek islands such as Crete, Karpathos and Rhodes. Greece's government insists that it possesses a comprehensive exclusive economic zone, or EEZ, around these islands and has already announced its opposition to the accord.

In a post on X, the Foreign Ministry announced that Greece firmly rejects the agreement and has the support of the European Union in doing so. "The European Union has described the Turkish-Libyan memorandum as null and void, stressing that it violates the sovereign rights of third states and is not consistent with the Law of the Sea," the ministry posted.

Similar wording came straight from Brussels. "I raised yesterday's Libyan vote on a maritime MoU with Türkiye and stressed that it infringes upon the sovereign rights of third states," Kaja Kallas, the European Union high representative for foreign affairs and security policy, posted on X.

Whose claims are legitimate in the eastern Mediterranean is the central point of contention. Greece primarily cites the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea, or UNCLOS. According to Article 121 of the convention, inhabited islands that "support an economic life of their own” have the same rights to an EEZ as the mainland. This applies to Crete, Rhodes and Lesbos.

Athens cites precedents such as a 2022 ruling by the International Court of Justicethat found that Colombia had violated the sovereign rights of Nicaragua by authorizing fishing activities in the Central American country's EEZ. The ICJ found that when interests overlap a fair demarcation must be reached that takes coastlines, geographical conditions and the implications of a potential boundary into account.

Turkey's government argues that the geography of the Mediterranean plays a special role: If every small Greek island off the coast of Turkey and Cyprus  were granted a full EEZ, Turkey would be largely cut off from access to the open sea. This, the government argues, must be given greater consideration when demarcating the boundary.

Unlike Greece, Turkey has never signed UNCLOS. Therefore, it does not agree to have the dispute heard by a UN arbitration tribunal. Moreover, even international experts on the law of the sea disagree on what a "fair division” might look like in this specific case.

When Turkey pushed for the agreement in 2019, a brutal civil war was still raging in Libya. At that time, Turkey intervened militarily in the west to support the government in Tripoli, thereby repelling the offensive by eastern Libyan General Khalifa Hifter.

Since then, the relationship has changed fundamentally: A successful charm offensive by Turkey's government appears to have led to a political rapprochement with Hifter and his camp. Turkey's government now maintains mutually advantageous relations with both parts of Libya. In addition to the countries' plan to develop energy resources in the Mediterranean together, Turkish construction companies have secured contracts worth billions in recent years for Libya's reconstruction. Furthermore, the Turkish state-owned oil company TPAO and the Libyan National Oil Corporation are already working on joint plans for seismic surveys off of Libya's coast.

Turkey's government has established itself as a new military partner for Hifter's forces, supplying weapons and offering training programs. In doing so, Turkey is pushing back against the influence of Egypt and Russia in eastern Libya and strengthening its role as a geopolitical player in the region.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

With the agreement now approved in the regional parliament, eastern Libya also aims to secure its share of the lucrative natural resources. This could give Hifter greater international influence in the future. Disputes in the eastern Mediterranean are likely to escalate further as a result of the agreement — particularly once Turkey and Libya begin extracting oil and gas from the disputed maritime area.

The agreement also jeopardizes a major project that is still in the planning stages: the EastMed pipeline, which is intended to transport natural gas from Israel and Cyprus via Greece, thereby making an important contribution to energy security in the European Union. However, the corridor established by Turkey and Libya cuts across the planned route of the pipeline. Turkey has also argued that projects involving cables or pipelines on its claimed continental shelf require its consent.

In the long term, this could lead to a division of the eastern Mediterranean region into two competing energy and defense blocs: The Turkey-Libya axis might be countered by an EU-supported alliance of Greece, Cyprus, Egypt and Israel.

#280. Apple AirTags (2nd Gen) Are on Sale for 20% Off Right Now

I never really understood the hype behind Apple AirTag trackers until I started testing them for work. I've since started singing their praises to anyone who will listen. Right now, a second-generation AirTag can be yours for just $23. They usually sell for $29 apiece, and while this deal price isn't exceedingly rare, it does match the lowest discount we've tracked. You can also get a four-pack for $89, a deal with a similar story but one that will save you some money if you want multiples.

AirTags are Bluetooth trackers that can be used to add anything to Apple's Find My app. My fellow Reviews team members love using these quarter-sized gadgets for tracking suitcases down while traveling. In the event that your luggage is left behind due to an airline error, as happened to editor Kat Merck, you'll be able to go straight to customer service rather than trying to track it down in a panic. Or, in my case, you can stash one in your car to remember which block you parked on a few days ago when it's time to move your vehicle again. Pop one on your keys, your diaper bag, your purse, or your dog's collar, and you'll be able to track the AirTag's location from your Find My app.

The batteries last for months and are easy to replace. The AirTag also has an impressively loud speaker inside, so you can ping your item's location with an audible alert. You can even share its location with airlines or your contacts so others can help you find something if it's misplaced.

Peace of mind is priceless, but today you can pick it up for $23. You might want to consider an AirTag holder, too—my buying guide is coming soon, but I use this option from Nomad for my keychain and like it a lot.

#279. British politicians among international delegates barred from entering West Bank

Group was on visit to meet Palestinian children, farmers and civil leaders facing escalating violence from Israeli forces

An international delegation including British politicians has been barred from entering the West Bank on a trip to meet Palestinian children, farmers and society leaders facing escalating violence from Israeli forces during olive harvest season.

The delegation of 23 people, including four elected representatives, was drawn from countries including France, Spain, Luxembourg, Switzerland, Greece, Holland, Ireland and the US. It included the Labour MP Clive Lewis, the Scottish National party MP Lara Bird, the Scottish Greens MSP Kate Nevens and the former Labour MP Sam Tarry.

Lewis told the Guardian: “We were detained, taken aside and then interrogated. I use the word interrogated because people were shouted at. We were asked why we were going in, what was happening, why our stories were one way or the other. The bottom line was it was a deeply unpleasant experience.”

The delegates were set to participate in Progressive International’s Second Ramallah Congress, a gathering to oppose Israeli occupation in Palestine. The congress was organised by Progressive International in partnership with Birzeit University and includes a programme of meetings with Palestinian academics, students and civil society organisations.

Tarry told the Guardian that he thought the delegation had a “genuine chance of going across” into the West Bank but they were detained at the Allenby/King Hussein Bridge by Israeli forces in Jordan for nine hours. “They made a political decision very clearly based on the fact they didn’t want people to witness what’s been going on in the West Bank, especially ahead of the elections,” he added.

The exclusions occurred amid attacks by Israeli forces on Palestinian olive farmers and foreign press and activists in the region. International travel to parts of the West Bank is permitted but subject to military checkpoints. Earlier this month, Israeli media reported that the defence minister, Israel Katz, had instructed officials to pursue exclusion orders against activists travelling to Palestinian olive-harvesting areas.

In the first two weeks of October, the UN aid coordination office recorded at least 160 Israeli settler attacks linked to the olive harvest, often under the protection or with the participation of Israeli forces.

Israeli forces attacked European diplomats and a French mayor accompanying Palestinian farmers, while about 80 olive-harvest volunteers, including US congressional candidate Brad Lander, were also recently detained.

Bird said in a statement: “I came to stand alongside Palestinians being attacked for harvesting olives on their own land. Instead, we were turned away at the border with no reason given.

“It is more important now than ever that we bear witness and show our solidarity. But we were prevented from doing so. If Israel has nothing to hide, why is it so determined to stop us seeing what is happening in the occupied West Bank?”

Laila Hazaineh, a political coordinator for Progressive International, said in a statement: “Despite escalating violence, our delegation remained determined to cross to Palestine to participate in the olive harvest, and actively engage with Palestinian communities and institutions. Every refusal of entry will only strengthen this determination.”

The embassy of Israel has been contacted for comment.

The airline has struggled not just with higher costs but also with competition from larger rivals.

It sought to acquire now-defunct carrier Spirit Airlines, but that deal was upended by a federal judge who ruled it was anticompetitive. #273. In Bulgaria, record low voter turnout triggers a November 1 presidential run-off in a poll colored by divisions over Ukraine war

Low voter turnout has triggered a run-off for the Bulgarian presidency. The vote was colored by strong divisions over Russia's war in Ukraine, and Sofia's relations with the Kremlin.

Record low turnout in Sunday's Bulgarian elections mean voters are expected to head to the polls on November 1 to cast ballots in a presidential run-off.

Polling stations opened at 7 a.m. and closed at 8 p.m. (0500-1800 GMT), with official results expected within 48 hours, but voter turnout was less than 40%, far short of the 50% required.

Voters were choosing between candidates with vastly different views of the war in Ukraine and relations with Moscow and the vote came as drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea off the Bulgarian coast brought home the significance of the conflict in Ukraine for the former Soviet satellite state.

Although the presidency is largely ceremonial, the head of state in Bulgaria can appoint interim governments during political crises, is commander-in-chief of the armed forces and is able to veto legislation.

Frontrunner in the elections is incumbent President Iliana Yotova, who took over the position this year after current Prime Minister Rumen Radev stepped down to take part in the parliamentary elections that led him to power.

Yotova, 62, who surveys show as having 41-47% of the vote, favors dialogue with Moscow as the way to bring peace to Ukraine and opposes military aid to Kyiv.

A win for Yotova would give a boost for Radev, who also advocates dialogue with Moscow. Victory would make Yotova Bulgaria’s first elected female president.

Her main challenger is Andrey Gyurov, a former caretaker prime minister, who is polling at 28-31%.

Gyurov, 50, who is backed by the pro-Western opposition, is an outspoken critic of Radev's foreign policy and strongly supports the Euro-Atlantic ties of Bulgaria, a European Union and NATO member state. As caretaker prime minister, Gyurov signed a security cooperation agreement with Kyiv.

The leader of the pro-Russian far-right Vazrazhdane party, Kostadin Kostadinov, is in third place on 10-13%.

Altogether 27 pairs of candidates for president and vice president are competing in the first round, with nearly half of them openly pro-Kremlin.

After casting her ballot in Sofia, Yotova called for peace, stability and unity, urging an end to divisions among Bulgarians. Her main challenger, Gyurov, voted in Blagoevgrad, calling for a Bulgaria that looks ahead and offers families greater economic security. Turnout was estimated at 15.1% by 3 p.m. local time (1300 GMT), according to polling agency Myara, Bulgarian media reported.

To win outright, a candidate must secure more than half the valid votes, with more than half of eligible voters taking part. Otherwise, the top two candidates advance to a runoff on November 1.

The war in Ukraine triggered by Russia's unprovoked invasion is one of the central themes in the election, particularly after four merchant ships were allegedly hit by drones off Bulgaria's coast this month in suspected spillover from the conflict. The attacks have prompted Bulgaria to seek assistance from its allies to strengthen maritime security.

Satellite internet antennas from the Starlink network — an important part of Ukraine's defense capabilities — were also set on fire near the capital, Sofia.

While Radev has said that the attacks seemed to be to the advantage of Russia and summoned the Russian ambassador, his government has declined to blame the incidents directly on Moscow.

The strikes have nonetheless raised concerns over Russia’s intentions to escalate the conflict.

Rising energy prices and inflation that have caused economic hardship to many in Bulgaria, the EU's poorest member state, are also a main issue addressed by candidates.

Many voters are also looking to stop government corruption, a persistent concern in the country, and hope for a stable government after multiple elections and coalition breakdowns in recent years.

#271. Jordan attacks militants in Syria in retaliation for church attack

Jordanian air force says it carried out strikes against ‘terrorist groups’ tied to killing of nine during funeral

Jordan has carried out airstrikes against armed groups in Syria in retaliation for Friday’s attack on a Greek Orthodox church in the southern Jordanian governorate of Karak that killed nine people, the Jordanian air force has said in a statement.

It said the operation “resulted in the killing and wounding of many members of these terrorist groups and the destruction of a number of their vehicles”. It did not specify who the groups were or the precise location of the strikes.

Syrian state media reported that civilians had been killed in the strikes. The director of health in the southern province of Daraa, where the strikes took place, told the Syrian national news agency, Sana, that at least five civilians had been killed, including women and children and a 65-year-old man.

Jordan’s armed forces said the operation was conducted in coordination with Damascus, but Syria’s government gave a contradictory account, saying it had launched an investigation into the ⁠Jordanian attacks, and stressed in a statement the importance of field and security coordination in border areas.

In the aftermath of the strikes, Syria said it had closed the ⁠Nassib border crossing with Jordan, suspending ⁠traffic in both directions. Nassib is Jordan’s only passenger and commercial border crossing with Syria.

Earlier on Monday, Jordanian authorities arrested three men for last week’s attack, which also wounded more than two dozen people. The arrests followed a dawn shootout, and all three men were wounded, officials said.

The attack happened during a funeral at St George’s Greek Orthodox church in Hamoud, Karak governorate, on Friday. Karak is home to a minority Christian population.

The attack shocked the nation, and Jordan declared three days of mourning for the victims. No group immediately claimed responsibility for the attack; the attackers’ motive remained unknown.

Later on Monday, Jordan’s Public Security Directorate identified the three arrested men as brothers Hazzah Zaidan Mohammad and Mishal Zaidan Mohammad, and their brother-in-law Ahmad Sami Hussein. All three are Jordanian; they were taken into custody in Karak, about 55 miles (90km) south of Amman.

The agency’s chief described the gunmen as “takfiri”, a term used for Sunni militants, but said they were not tied to any organised group.

“They only represent themselves,” Maj Gen Obaidallah Abd Rabbo Al-Maayta told a news conference. He also said a large cache of munitions was found in the abandoned house where Jordanian forces apprehended the men.

Jordan’s interior minister, Mazen al-Faraya, said the attack by the three men was the result of “a terroristic, extremist way of thinking”.

“If this way of thinking is not resolved, we will see incidents like this again,” he told reporters.

Jordan’s King Abdullah II had ordered authorities to respond with “an iron fist” against what he called the outlaws of Islam and anyone attempting to undermine the unity of the Jordanian people.

“We vowed the terrorists would not escape punishment, and no proponent of dark ideologies that divide Jordanians will be tolerated,” he said in a statement on Monday.

Relatives of the victims said the arrests brought them some relief.

“Despite the profound and immense tragedy, what happened today has eased our grief, and the grief of the Jordanian people – not just for us, the families of the martyrs,” Khaled al-Borqan, himself a survivor of the attack and a relative of three of the people killed, told Al-Mamlaka.

“Despite the sadness and pain, the news brought us joy,” Fares Halaseh, an uncle of one of the victims and a survivor of the attack, told the TV station.

But the pain remains, he added. “We will not forget this agony for years to come.”

This article was amended on 27 October 2026. The attacked church is located in Hamoud, in the Karak governorate, not in the city of Karak as stated in an earlier version.

#268. The 8 best 65-inch TVs to buy in 2026, from budget sets to stunning OLEDs

Compared to choosing a new robot vacuum or deciding between Ninja appliances, the journey to buy a new TV is arguably the most complex of the staple household electronics. You can technically go to a store to assess brightness, contrast, or size in person. But is seeing 15 TVs playing the same loop in an electronics store actually more helpful than it is disorienting?

If you're overwhelmed with all the options — QLED, OLED, MicroRGB, Mini LED — then we're here to help. To preface, we've dubbed 65 inches as the sweet spot for most people — a 65-inch screen is large enough to provide an immersive viewing experience yet practical enough to work on a TV stand or the wall. So, after testing a variety of TVs and consulting some of our Ziff Davis colleagues at CNET and PCMag, we're ready to make our top picks for 2026.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Unlike virtually every other piece of consumer technology, the best flatscreen TVs have actually gotten less expensive in recent years. It's actually pretty incredible how affordable these devices have gotten. That being said, a new flagship TV can still cost upward of $3,000. Fortunately, you don't have to pay full price.

During October and November, retailers like Amazon, Best Buy, Walmart, and Target all offer aggressive discounts on TVs. Starting on Oct. 6 with Amazon's Prime Big Deal Days sale, and continuing through Black Friday and Cyber Monday, you'll soon be able to save up to $1,200 on new TVs.

In addition, you can find big discounts by shopping directly with the manufacturers themselves. Samsung, in particular, hosts good sales during the fall and winter seasons.

Sony has long been revered as one of the best TV brands, and its Bravia TVs have been no strangers to lists of the best TVs across the internet. But Sony as a TV brand is about to look a lot different.

As of January 2026, TCL is in charge of Sony's home entertainment business, which spans TVs and home audio. The "Bravia" family of TVs isn't going anywhere, at least as far as branding and picture processing — but TCL will be behind much of the manufacturing and pricing. Sony TVs often toe the line of being so premium that they're too expensive for the average person to consider. But paired with TCL's budget-friendly expertise, they could make a wicked pairing come 2027.

The TCL QM6K is the quintessential beginner fancy TV between the absolute cheapest and most expensive QLEDs out there. Compared to regular LED TVs, the QM6K is brighter and sharper — better for daytime viewing and watching sports. Compared to more premium mini LED TVs, the QM6K stays comfortably under the $1,000 mark.

Low input lag and an impressive suite of gaming features make it a great choice for gamers who want something in between, too.

It's always mind-blowing that the TCL QM6K doesn't cost more than it does. You won't find a more fluid picture or better black levels in a 65-inch at $649.99 (the QM6K's frequent sale price). Measuring just over two inches thick, the QM6K is also impressively thin for this price range.

I recently upgraded to this model in my own apartment after letting myself complain about my old TV's inability to pop for years too long. My living room gets a ton of natural light, so I needed something that was comfortably bright enough for early afternoon football games or getting the full colorful The Price Is Right experience at 11 a.m., just like my grandma taught me. Huge news: Football fields are actually green rather than having a sepia tint. Groundbreaking.

The QM6K also has great dark room performance for the price. I watch a new scary movie at least one night per week and am very serious about Game of Thrones and its spinoffs (all notoriously dark and shadowy). It's been awesome to not have to guess what's going on in a low-light nighttime scene.

The QM6K is able to hit those marks because it doesn't bank on an edge-lit display like many cheap mini LED TVs do. Its Precise Dimming feature alters the lighting of 500 zones of tiny LED bulbs across the entire screen, updating by the second as the content shifts.

With superb peak brightness, detailed picture quality, and fantastic glare handling, the QM8L would be best for people who know they'll be doing a good amount of their TV watching in the daytime (especially if it's sports).

The TCL QM8L offers great contrast and blooming mitigation in dark rooms for a non-OLED TV. And its non-OLED pricetag will be enticing to shoppers who are more than casual movie watchers but not exactly strict cinephiles with no budget cap. Each size of the TCL QM8L can often be found on sale for $1,000+ off, bringing both the 65-inch and 75-inch models under $2,000 — not the absolute cheapest you can go by any means, but certainly on the affordable side compared to flagship OLEDs from LG, Samsung, and Sony.

"What is the best QLED TV?" is a loaded question if no other prerequisites are provided, given the sheer variety of TVs under the QLED umbrella nowadays. But if we must generalize, the TCL QM8L's value is pretty hard to beat, period.

The peak brightness achieved here is sublime, clocking up to 6,000 nits. That easily makes the QM8L one of the brightest TVs you can buy. That's also two times the brightness of the base-tier QM7L, which would only save you around $200 if both models are on sale. It's not uncommon to find the 65-inch QM8L for $1,499.99 — a full $1,000 off its debut price.

Compared to the 2025 QM8K, the QM8L has a completely revamped backlight system: Super Quantum Dots. The nanocrystals in SQD TVs are a fraction of the size of traditional quantum dots: five nanometers compared to 60. Their microscopic size narrows each crystal's spectral response, making each significantly more precise in the color it reflects.

The concept of SQD may hurt your brain in a "comparing the size of Earth to a massive star" way. But the result in both bright and dark rooms is unambiguous. When you're getting this nitty-gritty, lifelike contrast in any room lighting looks easy. And the chances of haloing are slim to none.

Budget OLED TVs aren't really a thing, but the LG C6 is the best affordable OLED TV given its competitors' prices. From inky black levels to a wide viewing angle, the C6 delivers an exceptional theater experience in dark rooms — even to folks sitting on the end of the couch. Compared to the LG C5, the C6 is slightly brighter and better for gaming.

LG's C series OLEDs are the darling of the TV-obsessed corner of the internet, and the 2026 C6 might just be the most accessible one yet. The 65-inch model can often be found on sale for just $1,999.99 — $1,300 less than the sale price of the 2026 Samsung S95H OLED.

But the LG C6 is still premium in so many ways. As we've grown to expect from the C series, the C6's wheelhouse is made up of stark contrast, crisp deep blacks, and generally impeccable image quality. It builds on the 2025 C5 with higher peak brightness, a more vibrant yet more natural color palette, and a refresh rate boost from 144Hz to 165Hz.

If you already have a 65-inch C5, it's probably not worthwhile to bump to the C6. Only the 77- and 83-inch C6H models received the mega panel upgrade that includes LG's Hyper Radiant Color Tech and up to three times the brightness of the B Series.

It's hard to find a situation where the Samsung S95H won't excel, but its elite contrast really dazzles in dark rooms. Its native 165Hz refresh rate can keep up with high-speed gaming better than most.

The S95H has the best bright room performance of any OLED TV in 2026. That's partially thanks to Samsung's anti-glare coating. With a unique silver bezel and special mount that lies flush with the wall, the S95H speaks to shoppers who want an art TV that can actually double as a home theater TV or gaming TV.

The steep price tag is the main disadvantage of the S95H, cementing this model as a pick for seasoned OLED owners who are willing to pay for the most advanced picture quality. Even on sale, it's still over $1,000 steeper than the LG C6 on sale.

Let's say your TV time is truly evenly split between daytime sports, graphics-heavy gaming, and nighttime movies with a strict focus on cinematography. Finding a TV that can legitimately keep up with all three is a tall order, but the 2026 Samsung S95H OLED nails it. PCMag can't help but rank this as the very best TV you can buy in 2026.

The S95H's contrast ratio is as close to infinite as you could ask for. The black levels are deep and the shadows are intricate to the point of feeling lifelike. In lit rooms, the S95H's peak brightness of up to 2,500 nits is a new benchmark breakthrough, according to PCMag's Will Greenwald.

While the S95H isn't officially an art TV, it has major art TV energy — it beats Samsung's own The Frame Pro in just about every category. Samsung's flagship OLED TVs are esteemed reflection handlers, using a best-in-class anti-glare coating to appear matte rather than glossy. But the physical design upgrade here is the most outwardly art TV-like upgrade of all. The S95H ditches the classic black bezel for a silver frame and comes with a floating wall mount that can lie flat against the wall. With access to the Samsung Art Store thrown in, the S95H checks the three main art TV boxes.

Sony introduced its new "True RGB" flagship TVs earlier this year, and the company was keen to distinguish between its RGB display technology and the competition. According to Sony's spokespeople, many so-called RGB TVs (variously branded as Micro RGB, RGB Mini LED, and others) aren't deserving of the name. Rather than using a standard LED and color filter, Sony's True RGB display uses actual RGB mini-LED backlighting to create a more dazzling array of colors. The TV is impressively bright (like OLED displays before it), and thanks to an advanced processor and Google Gemini, this is a smart TV in every sense of the word.

While our colleagues at PCMag found some quirks (side-angle viewing isn't flawless, and they prefer classic OLED to the new RGB displays), they also noted that this TV is a better option for gamers than Sony's or LG's flagship TVs.

To put it simply: This TV is gorgeous. It's a stunning addition to any living room or home theater, whether installed on the wall or on a stand. The True RGB display delivers beautiful colors and brightness, though some people may prefer a more traditional OLED display. (If you prefer OLED or don't want to gamble on a newer display technology, Samsung's incredible S55H OLED TV is equally stunning, plus it comes with some Art Mode extras.)

If you like the Google TV OS or regularly use Gemini, then you'll appreciate that this TV is powered by Google Gemini. You'll also appreciate the impressive spatial audio system. Finally, because of the next-gen processor and extremely low input lag, it's a great pair for a Sony PlayStation 5 console.

Of course, there is the price tag to consider. However, if you're looking for a future-proofed TV with a stunning display and overall design, this is a more-than-worthy investment.

Samsung's The Frame has a pretty specific target audience: folks who want a realistic digital picture frame or cohesive piece of wall art first and a decent TV second. The Frame is the slimmest art TV out there, measuring just a hair over an inch thick.

The Frame may not be as bright as The Frame Pro, but it's definitely a better value overall. The 2025 65-inch model can regularly be found on sale for less than $1,000.

Well, I have to mention the classic art TV in this list — its massive popularity says a lot about how well Samsung has perfected the design over the years.

Cinephiles aren't going to be thrilled with The Frame's limited contrast. But mega TV heads probably aren't the main market for a premium art TV that takes the movie displaying part less seriously, anyway. We can save the art of making a filmed movie look like you're witnessing it IRL to OLEDs. The Frame is much better at making you feel like you're looking at a legitimate piece of framed artwork, museum style.

When trying to decide between The Frame and The Frame Pro, we can't blindly base the decision on backlighting. The Frame Pro may be brighter, but its mini LED lighting isn't full-array local dimming. That means it's not achieving the same precise contrast created by dimming zones, where clusters of individually-controlled LEDs are arranged across the entire screen. In terms of art TVs, the cheaper non-Pro version of The Frame makes better use of your money. And if you really want the full vibrant mini LED experience, there are other slim mini LEDs out there that'll actually dazzle you.

Compared to the 2024 and 2026 versions, The Frame from 2025 is the best bang for your buck. The 2026 model (LS03HE) has a faster processor, an even more matte display, and richer color accuracy through Pantone Validation. But there's a huge $800 difference between the average sale prices of the 65-inch 2026 and 2025 The Frame, and the newest model's tweaks aren't major enough to justify that.

RGB TVs approach backlighting in an entirely different way than any of the other types of TV. They're phenomenal when watching in a sunlit space or dark room, bringing a whole new meaning to "mixed usage TV."

The Hisense UR9 is the best way for most people to bring the newest TV technology home. It's bright and saturated with commendable contrast for a non-OLED model, but is still a bit more affordable than the most premium options from other brands. The UR9 also sets itself apart with an ultra-slim build and DisplayPort, an advanced gaming input typically found on monitors.

Samsung, Sony, LG, and Hisense have all taken part in the RGB TV boom of 2026. Nearly 10 totally new models hit the market in the span of just a few months, but the Hisense UR9 manages to stand out in a few ways — and not just because it's the slimmest of them all.

First and foremost, RGB TVs are known for their color palettes. RGB backlights source light directly from thousands of tiny red, green, and blue LEDs, rather than the traditional method of reflecting white or blue LEDs through an LCD panel. These true light sources supply highly saturated hues with extreme precision. They can fluoresce 100 percent coverage of the color gamut, or the full possible spectrum of colors visible to humans.

On top of its ethereal color performance, the UR9's local dimming feature achieves deep black levels that aren't too far off from what you'd get from an OLED TV. But the UR9 isn't as limited in a bright room as most OLEDs. The 65-inch model can reach a peak brightness of up to 5,000 nits. It's a solid pick for taking advantage of HDR.

For serious PC gamers, the UR9 is a rare premium TV with full DisplayPort Alt Mode functionality. This boosts the display's maximum refresh rate to 180Hz at 4K (or up to 330Hz at lower resolutions).

Gamers who also want a TV that blends in with their gallery wall should heavily consider the Hisense CanvasTV over either of Samsung's options. The Canvas has a 144Hz refresh rate compared to The Frame's 120Hz, and has proven to have noticeably less input lag than The Frame. Though The Frame Pro has a slight edge over the Canvas specs-wise, the Canvas is easily the better value for any shoppers who have some semblance of a budget cap.

The OG Samsung Frame is the obvious less expensive alternative to its Pro sibling. We probably didn't need to tell you that. But the regular Frame shouldn't automatically be your go-to if you want an art TV on the cheaper side. Despite often being minimized to a Frame dupe, the Hisense Canvas is actually a better value than The Frame in a lot of ways: Notably, it has a 144Hz refresh rate over the Frame's 120Hz and two HDMI 2.1 ports over the Frame's lone 2.1 port, both of which make it the better budget art TV for gaming by far.

The array of more-advanced features makes the Canvas's price point even more surprising, especially on sale. The 65-inch Hisense CanvasTV has been spotted as low as $699.99 — the exact same sale price as the 55-inch version of the same TV. The 65-inch The Frame has never dropped that low.

Deputy Shopping Editor Miller Kern has a Hisense CanvasTV hanging in a gallery wall of actual framed art, and this thing is chic. "The CanvasTV has a matte finish that makes it look less like a TV and more like a giant picture frame. It's not totally glare-proof, but it does look really nice whether the TV is on or off. The TV itself looks stupid good in a gallery wall and really does blend in with other artwork and wall hangings, especially in art mode," she said.

If you do want to display real paintings instead of your own photos, Hisense lets you access its digital art library for free, whereas a Samsung Art Store subscription costs $4.99 per month. Use the VIDAA Art App to choose from over 1,000 pieces from genres like abstract, Renaissance, and modern.

Most of us will stumble across the same fork in the road on the quest to find the best 4K TV for our personal watching, gaming, and lighting situation. The options: Falling into a Reddit rabbit hole about the best TV to buy in 2026 or just seeing what an expert has to say.

TV specs are notoriously confusing to people who don't test them for a living. Luckily, we happen to know some people who actually do test TVs for a living: Ty Pendlebury, CNET's TV and home video editor, and Will Greenwald, PCMag's home theater and AR/VR expert. CNET and PCMag are both owned by Mashable's publisher, Ziff Davis, and both have dedicated labs for testing TVs.

Leah Stodart is a Philadelphia-based Senior Shopping Reporter at Mashable where she covers and tests essential home tech like vacuums, TVs, beauty devices, and eco-friendly hacks. Her ever-evolving experience in these categories helps her make thoughtful recommendations for how to spend your money during shopping holidays like Black Friday, which Leah has been covering for Mashable since 2017.

#265. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#262. Prague honors Roma resistance fighters against Hitler

More than 1,400 Roma fought against the German Nazi occupation in Czechoslovakia. A new exhibition in Prague showcases the stories of partisans and soldiers whose fates barely received any attention for decades.

For more than half a million Roma in Czechia and Slovakia, October 15, 2026, marks an important milestone.

That day, an exhibition on a largely forgotten chapter of Czech history opened at the Museum of the Romani National Revival in Prague. The exhibit explores the role of Czech and Slovak Romani resistance fighters during the Nazi German occupation and the dismantling of Czechoslovakia from 1938 to 1945.

Until now, Czech and Slovak Roma appeared in the history of World War II almost exclusively as victims of the Holocaust against Roma and Sinti. In the so-called Protectorate of Bohemia and Moravia, Nazi Germany murdered approximately 90% of the Czech Roma during World War II.

However, what is less well known is that a large number of Czech and Slovak Roma fought against the German occupiers as partisans in resistance groups and as soldiers in the Czechoslovak Underground Army, or Defense of the Nation, ON.

The exhibition at the Museum of the Roma National Movement therefore marks a turning point in how the Roma are viewed during World War II. At least that is the hope of museum director Emil Scuka, a lawyer, former prosecutor and one of the best-known Czech Roma activists.

"We were not only victims; we were also heroes," Scuka said in his speech to several hundred attendees at the exhibition opening, most of whom were Roma from Czechia and Slovakia. "Many people, not even among the Roma themselves, are aware that we Roma fought against Nazism," he noted. In his view, "organizing and opening the exhibition was something my generation owed to these Roma heroes and today we are repaying that debt."

Scuka called on those present: "Bring your children and grandchildren here so they can see for themselves and realize that the Roma have heroes too!"

He also pointed out that this topic had been completely marginalized in Czech and Slovak historical writing for decades and that the Roma had therefore decided on their own to document the fates of their war heroes.

The exhibition presents the detailed life stories of 100 of the most significant Roma partisans and Roma soldiers from the Czech underground army and resistance groups who fought against the Nazis.

"We found over 1,400 Romani fighters in the archives. From among them, we selected the 100 most interesting stories for this exhibition," said exhibition curator Radoslav Scuka at the opening.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Among these figures is Anton Facuna, a sergeant in the American intelligence agency Office of Strategic Services (OSS), who was flown to Slovakia in October 1944 on a secret mission. Slovakia had been a puppet state of Hitler from 1939 to 1945.

Another is Josef Serinek, known by the nickname "Black Partisan from Vysocina," who managed to escape from the Nazi concentration camp for Roma in Lety and subsequently founded and commanded the large Capajev-Cerny partisan unit in the Bohemian-Moravian Highlands.

In 2022, Czech President Milos Zeman posthumously awarded him the Medal for Heroism. Toward the end of his life, Serinek shared his memories with historian Jan Tesar, who later wrote the three-volume book "Ceska cikanska rapsodie", which translates to Czech Gypsy Rhapsody.

Except for Tesar's book, the history of Roma resistance against Nazism has not been researched by Czech and Slovak historians to this day. "I am not aware of any scholarly historical work that deals with Roma resistance against Nazism," Prokop Tomek, a historian at the Military History Institute in Prague, told DW.

"The number of 1,400 Roma who took part in the fight against Nazism as members of the resistance or in the foreign army does not strike me as an exaggeration and corresponds to the proportion of the Roma population in Czechoslovakia," Tomek said, adding that the "exhibition on the Roma resistance against Nazism during the war is undoubtedly groundbreaking."

To this day, many Roma in Czechia and Slovakia do not publicly acknowledge their ethnic identity. This is partly due to experiences with the Holocaust during the Nazi era that have been passed down through generations. Czech historians and politicians continue to debate the extent to which the Czech authorities of the Protectorate were complicit in the extermination of the Czech Roma.

However, one thing is certain: the police registration of all Roma, which had been enshrined in law in Czechoslovakia since 1927, facilitated the extermination of the Roma by the German Nazis.

According to estimates by the Czech government, approximately 250,000 Roma live in the country today. In the 2021 census, however, only 21,000 people identified as Roma.

Of all ethnic groups in Czechia, which has a population of 10 million, the Roma are among the three largest ethnic minorities in terms of numbers. However, they are the most socially disadvantaged, have very little political representation, and are therefore scarcely present in Czech politics.

This article was originally written in German.

#261. Fears grow for Iranian athletes on death row as mountaineer latest to be hanged

Two sportspeople have been executed in the past month and at least six more face the same fate, says activist group

Fears are growing for more Iranian athletes facing execution after mountain climber Ali-Sina Mostafavi became the latest in a string to be killed by the regime.

Mostafavi – who documented his ascents of some of the world’s highest peaks, including Mount Everest – was executed on 14 October having been arrested in April after the anti-government protests a few months earlier. His death followed the killing of Siavash Jamshidi Kheirabadi, a taekwondo athlete, at the start of October, and the mixed martial arts fighter Gholamreza Khani Shakarab, who was hanged in May.

Now Human Rights for Sport – a group of former Iranian athletes based in Oslo that has been documenting the growing number of cases – has warned that at least six more are facing execution after having their sentences upheld by Iran’s supreme court. They include the swimmer Ali Pishevarzadeh and the footballer Ehsan Hosseinpour, who were both arrested during the January protests, and Mohammad Javad Vafaei Sani – a boxer arrested in 2020 for taking part in protests a year earlier. He saw his request for a retrial rejected last year.

Another MMA fighter, Benyamin Naghdi, equestrian athlete Taraneh Rahimi and the climber Maryam Siri have also been condemned to death this year.

Human Rights for Sport said six athletes have already been executed in cases linked to the January protests or earlier political charges.

Dr Zohreh Abdollahkhani of Human Rights for Sport, said: “The Iranian sporting community is under enormous pressure. For one of those sentenced to death, we managed to raise attention and awareness through other organisations and they halted his execution. But it was only temporary and I’m very concerned about all of them.”

Human Rights for Sport believe that athletes have been targeted to send a message to the wider community, with the deaths of prominent sportspeople often given widespread coverage in the state-run media. They include Navid Afkari Sangari – a wrestler who was executed in September 2020.

“He was a role model and very famous in the whole community. What happened to him was also a warning to the whole population: look what we can do to someone like this,” Abdollahkhani said.

Mostafavi’s death sentence was upheld by the supreme court at the end of September and he is believed to have been subjected to physical violence during his detention in Kerman before being transferred to solitary confinement. He was accused of being an “armed agent of the American-Zionist enemy” and was arrested for allegedly opening fire on civilians in Azadi Square in Kerman on 24 March and taking part in January’s protests. Mostafavi’s family are thought to have been summoned to the prison hours before his execution.

Abdollahkhani said that making contact with those facing the death sentence is a challenge, as some members of their families are also being targeted by security services.

“In one case, his sister and brother-in-law have been arrested and the whole family’s phone lines are under surveillance. So it’s simply impossible to make direct contact,” she said.

Overall, Human Rights for Sport has found that 217 Iranian athletes were killed by direct gunfire in January, and more than 100 others detained. Four members of the European parliament also wrote to Fifa before the World Cup concerning Iranian athletes facing execution but Abdollahkhani said they received no response.

“Fifa is aware of the concerns raised regarding the situation of Iranian athletes and thanks all stakeholders for their continued collaboration and dedication to raising awareness of these critical matters,” a spokesperson for football’s governing body said.

Abdollahkhani also criticised the International Olympic Committee (IOC), saying changes in staffing had made it difficult to raise cases through its human rights and safeguarding structures. The IOC disputed her account, saying it had “fully functioning human rights and safeguarding teams”. It said it was deeply concerned about athletes affected by events in Iran but lacked the authority, as a non-governmental organisation, to change a sovereign country’s laws or political system.

“Within our remit, we work tirelessly with all stakeholders of the Olympic movement, including national Olympic committees and international federations, to support the creation of a safe sporting environment for all athletes. We will continue to work with our Olympic stakeholders to support all athletes globally through sports diplomacy, and we remain in touch with the Olympic community of Iran.”

#256. GM will switch to Nasdaq from the NYSE on Nov. 17

DETROIT — General Motors plans to transfer shares of its common stock from the New York Stock Exchange to the Nasdaq as the automaker attempts to position itself more as a technology-focused company.

The Detroit automaker said Tuesday that it expects to begin trading as a Nasdaq-listed company on Nov. 17. Its ticker symbol is expected to remain "GM," the company said.

"GM's customer-focused, technology-driven strategy is creating value for our stockholders, making Nasdaq a natural home for our listing as we grow," GM CFO Paul Jacobson said in a release. "We're grateful for our long-standing partnership with the New York Stock Exchange and its remarkable legacy."

GM noted the move will provide eligibility for potential inclusion in the Nasdaq-100 Index.

Current shareholders do not need to take any action with respect to the transfer, the company said.

The switch marks a notable loss for the NYSE, as GM first listed on the exchange in 1911. The Detroit automaker also relisted on the stock exchange in 2010 following a government-backed bankruptcy.

GM follows other companies such as Walmart, DoorDash and Palantir Technologies that have transferred from the more traditional NYSE to the historically technology focused Nasdaq in recent years. Hilton Worldwide Holdings last week also announced plans to move trading from the NYSE to Nasdaq.

The key drivers for a listing transfer differ from company to company, but the Nasdaq in 2024 said common reasons include "index inclusion opportunities, cost effective fee structure, brand visibility partnership, access to Nasdaq's event space, and Nasdaq's suite of investor relations and ESG products."

GM's decision comes a week after the automaker held a technology-focused event where executives laid out the company's roadmap for artificial intelligence and self-driving vehicle technologies.

Shares of GM, which has been aggressively buying back stock, have more than doubled since 2024. GM's stock closed Monday at $76.31 per share. The company's market cap is roughly $64.4 billion.

#254. Why Anthropic’s IPO Prospectus Is Missing So Many Key Figures

Anthropic filed its hotly anticipated IPO prospectus on Monday, spelling out parts of the company’s business, strategy, and risk profile ahead of its public debut. Though the filing, known as an S-1, is several hundred pages long, it has some notable omissions—namely, certain financial figures.

“Claude has experienced the fastest enterprise adoption of any technology in history, as demonstrated by revenue growing from $0 at commercial launch in the first quarter of 2023 to $_____ for the three months ended September 30, 2026,” reads the first of dozens of such examples.

It’s relatively common for companies to redact certain information in their S-1 filings, and Securities and Exchange Commission rules allow for a certain degree of leniency around the practice, particularly when it comes to information considered “confidential.”

Still, it’s helpful to know what, exactly, these companies decide to leave out. In Anthropic’s case, the missing details largely revolve around financials for its most recent quarter, which concluded at the end of September, and lease agreements tied to its compute investments. While experts expect many details to be disclosed before Anthropic goes public, their current absence may hint at the uncertainties of investing in artificial intelligence, as companies work to secure sufficient compute.

Anthropic did not respond to a request for comment.

Michael Dambra, an accounting and law professor at the University at Buffalo School of Management, tells WIRED that there are “practical reasons” for Anthropic’s third-quarter numbers to be absent: September 30 was only a few weeks ago, and “closing the books for a company of this size takes time.”

Companies can also be liable for misstatements in their registration statements, “so there is a strong incentive not to publish numbers before they have been fully vetted,” Dambra says. SEC rules require that “Anthropic would need to update the filing with third-quarter financials” if the company goes public any later than about mid-November, he says.

As for data center lease agreements, Erica Neuman, an accounting professor at Youngstown State University, says it’s “not unusual” for companies to redact confidential details from “contract exhibits,” which could include lease terms.

However, Neuman adds that it’s worth scrutinizing whether Anthropic’s redactions would prevent, or make it more difficult, for investors to analyze Anthropic’s financial obligations and whether they may impact the company’s cash flow.

Many of Anthropic’s financial obligations relate to compute, which is the “most significant operating cost in our business,” according to the S-1. It’s also a business risk for the company, and it’s laid out both in terms of undersupply and overinvestment. (Parts of the AI industry have been warning that chip constraints could slow the progress of AI.)

Anthropic’s prospectus puts more emphasis on the risks of overinvestment, stating that the company needs to make forward investments in compute resources that will seem “irresponsibly large” at first glance.

Chips, Anthropic explains, are approached as a “single, fungible pool from multiple strategic partners,” including Amazon, Google, Nvidia, and AMD. The company notes that it has entered into contracts “with an aggregate committed spend of more than $____, substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure.” Some of these contracts are set to be completed before December 2030, while others may extend through 2045.

Though the prospectus touches on Anthropic’s compute uncertainties due to factors like limited land and power resources, Patrick Moorhead, CEO and chief analyst at Moor Insights & Strategy, believes investors are owed more detail.

“It’s about characterizing the odds and impact individually across each of the areas. Anthropic owes investors more information, such as a probability of these individual issues, characterizing the percentage chance and the degree of the impact,” he tells WIRED. “Not ‘could materially impact.’”

Anthropic does provide some eye-catching estimates in footnotes. Since December 31, 2025, it has started or amended “compute and infrastructure” leases with “future minimum payment obligations” worth approximately $417 billion in aggregate. Since June 30, it has added an additional $245 billion to its future payment obligations, all related to infrastructure agreements. It also expanded “certain existing commercial agreements” that it caveats “have not yet been finalized,” for an additional $125 billion to $150 billion.

Include “short term” agreements with “minimum non-cancelable payments,” Anthropic says, and that’s another $21 billion. In total, it will owe, at minimum, an estimated $808 billion if it follows through on all of this.

Ben Bajarin, chief executive and a principal analyst at Creative Strategies who tracks the chip industry, says that this is likely just the first draft of Anthropic’s prospectus, and he expects a lot of gaps to be filled in an updated version.

Despite how common S-1 redactions are, Dambra describes them as “controversial.” While companies may argue that they’re “protecting proprietary information,” Dambra says that skeptics accuse them of using redactions “opportunistically” in order to “avoid disclosing politically or socially sensitive information.”

However, Dambra argues that redacting information may come at a cost. He notes that companies with redactions in their S-1s were also more likely to “underprice” their shares at the initial public offering.

When companies redact information in their S-1, Dambra says, they typically understand that they’ve created an “information gap” between them and outside investors. Understanding that investors will treat this gap with “suspicion,” companies may feel inclined to “entice” them to participate in the IPO by setting a lower starting price.

It’s unclear what Anthropic’s opening price may end up being or how investors may be interpreting the redactions in the company’s S-1. But according to Bajarin, the whole AI industry is one form of “blank” or another when it comes to crunching the numbers based on access to chips and infrastructure right now. So, yes, it’s all a risk.

“All of this,” he says, “just shows how absurdly early we are in enterprise adoption of an AI market that will be more than $3 trillion yearly and likely growing.”

#253. Legacy of 7 October looms large as a divided Israel goes to the polls in record numbers

Bereaved families tell voters to remember the victims while others have flown thousands of miles to cast their ballots

Election day in Israel began with a protest by some of the voters with most at stake. Bereaved families of those killed in the 7 October attacks held a minute of silence at 6.29 am, the moment when Hamas launched its onslaught just over three years ago.

They gathered in a Tel Aviv plaza that has become known as “hostages square” because it was the centre of demonstrations demanding the return of more than 200 captives from Gaza. Holding pictures of their loved ones, they stood in silence then called on voters to remember the losses and betrayals of that day when deciding on the next government.

“Three years after the massacre, our loved ones will not be voting today. Go out and vote – remember them at the ballot box. This is what these elections are about,” the group said.

They are not explicitly backing any political party, but one of their key demands is a state commission of inquiry into the attacks. Benjamin Netanyahu has resisted this and it has become a rallying point for the opposition parties trying to unseat him as prime minister.

Netanyahu’s strongest challenger, Gadi Eisenkot, started the day by visiting communities attacked on 7 October. “If someone in the government had a shred of responsibility, they would start the morning here,” said the former military chief and leader of the Yashar party.

Standing in a cheering crowd, Eisenkot called on all Israelis to vote to get rid of “the worst government Israel has known”. He noted that victory wasn’t certain, adding that “we are on perhaps the most critical day since the founding of the state”.

As the day wore on, it became clear that voter turnout was shattering all records and among those lining up at polling stations were tens of thousands of Israelis who live overseas.

A bipartisan “Fly&Vote” initiative was set up to support those who wanted to return because electoral law only allows voting in person. While not everyone coming back was opposed to the government, anecdotal evidence suggested many were opposition supporters who held the view that Israel faced an existential choice.

Monica Moria, 58, and her husband, Ron, flew in to Tel Aviv from Barcelona. They took a train to a kibbutz where she is registered to vote and she cast her ballot for the most leftwing of the Jewish Israeli parties, the Democrats.

She said: “It feels like Israel has lost its way, there are deep divisions and hatreds that will be very difficult to heal. The idea is to bring a political change so that we can bring Israeli society together again. I want to be optimistic, but I am also afraid.”

Kobi Mizrahi, 35, who has been completing a PhD in New York, paid more than $1,600 (£1,200) to come back to Israel for just a few days and vote.

He said: “Given the fact that it feels like a ‘now or never’ moment, where if we lose these elections to the Bibi coalition, this might be the turning point we can’t come back to. I felt like I have to do anything in my power to help.”

Yuval Schwarzfuchs, 26, travelled from Rio de Janeiro via Ethiopia to vote for the Palestinian-led Joint List. “For me, its about equality for all,” he said. “I did what I had to do.”

He came to the Jerusalem polling station wearing a T-shirt with an image of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, the leftwing president of Brazil, who defied the polls to win re-election at the weekend.

In the settlement of Efrat near Jerusalem, Shani, 19, had come to the polling station with her parents, to cast her vote for the far-right party of the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich.

She is part of a large cohort of first-time voters who are more rightwing than older generations and may help bolster support for Netanyahu and his far-right coalition partners.

More than 500,000 Israelis have reached voting age since the last election in 2022, and three-quarters of first-time Jewish voters define themselves as right or centre-right, a poll for Channel 12 television found (it did not survey Palestinian citizens of Israel).

Shani said she chose Smotrich, a settler himself, for his promises to expand Israel’s footprint in occupied Palestine. “Smotrich is pushing forward with settlement expansion and improving security,” she said.

Her mother, kindergarten teacher Rivka, also voted for Smotrich, and said her priority was to return Netanyahu’s coalition to office.

“The right must win, because otherwise there is a chance of a Palestinian state, which will be very bad for Jews,” she said.

Spiralling attacks by militant Israeli settlers on Palestinians, and the growing threat of sanctions on settlement goods and construction, were not a key election issue for voters in Efrat who spoke to the Guardian.

The Palestinian Israeli vote is a critical camp in this election, representing about a fifth of eligible voters. Their votes are expected to go to either Ra’am, led by Mansour Abbas, or Joint List, an electoral alliance of Palestinian-Israeli parties.

Even if the opposition bloc led by Eisenkot wins more seats than Netanyahu’s coalition, it is not expected to win an outright majority. It has also ruled out going into coalition with the Palestinian Israeli parties, which could well hold the balance of power.

Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, the leading party in the Joint List coalition, thinks Eisenkot’s approach may change after the elections.

“He cannot sit down [in coalition] with Netanyahu, and he won’t want to go back to another round of elections, so we think he can be flexible,” he said.

In Nazareth, the largest Palestinian-majority town in Israel, the turnout at polling stations has been noticeably higher than at recent elections, although still below the average Jewish turnout across the country. A surge of evening voting was expected here as people came home from work.

At the headquarters of the Joint List, the most pressing concern was the spoiler tactics of far-right election monitors at predominantly Palestinian polling stations.

“They can say that they want to check all the voter numbers and identity and everything, so they can slow things down, so that people wait a long time and give up. That’s their intention,” said Shbita.

At worst, these ploys have slowed the process down so that it took half an hour to vote, rather than a couple of minutes, he added.

Shbita said voters were sufficiently motivated to overcome such obstacles. “It is the first time for a long time that a government has stayed in office for its full term, four years,” he said.

“We are talking about one of the worst governments when it comes to Arab society. We feel racism at all levels of Israeli society, in hospitals, in buses, in public transportation, in the media. So people understand that today we have the opportunity to change the government.”
