#251. France: Hundreds arrested after nationwide protests

The French government says more than 1,000 people were arrested following nationwide protests on Saturday. Police and elite security forces were out in force across the country as clashes broke out.

This blog is now closed. Thank you for reading.

You can read all our updates on the protests and unrest in France and other parts of Europe on October 24 and 25, 2026, below.

Marine Le Pen launched her fourth bid to become president of France on Sunday with a plea to "French patriots" to back her and her far-right National Rally (RN) in spring elections.

The grande dame of the far-right, Le Pen delivered an impassioned invocation of French spirit in Orleans, calling for "a renaissance" of the country's virtues and stressing its rich creative and intellectual history.

She blasted "Little Marquis Macron" and the state of France under his stewardship — both domestically and internationally — offering a fiery outline of the need to restore order, but also civility in the country, alongside promises of maintaining French (nuclear) defense and judicial sovereignty.

Le Pen said "we'll protect our public spaces so that they can remain places full of life and mutual respect" and cited civility as the surest way to keep violence out of society.

She praised regionalism over globalism or supranationalism in the form of the European Union (EU), and spoke of the need to defend freedom and the "French way of life," making clear that, "In France, you live like the French."

Le Pen recalled General Charles de Gaulle several times throughout her speech, emphasizing the historic importance of the moment when it comes to securing the promise of the future.

After ending her remarks with a full-throated "Vive la France!," a relieved-looking Le Pen was joined on stage by heir apparent Jordan Bardella and party members for a rousing rendition of the national anthem under the campaign slogan, "Pour la France La Renaissance."

Amid the latest demonstrations, what's driving the unrest, and could a broader youth-led protest movement be taking shape?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

French unions and protest groups have called for fresh demonstrations on October 31 and a nationwide strike on November 5 to maintain pressure on the government.

The left-wing Solidaires union federation said anger over wages, living costs and public services was bringing together students, workers, firefighters and other groups.

"We see an aggregation of anger," Solidaires co-leader Murielle Guilbert told broadcaster BFM TV.

The SUD Education teaching union said it would join the October 31 demonstrations. The larger CGT union is set to decide during the week.

The Yellow Vests movement also called for rallies, including sit-ins outside town halls and the National Assembly.

The government has opened talks with unions and student representatives, but protest leaders say its proposals are inadequate.

French authorities detained 1,314 people during nationwide protests on Saturday, according to a provisional Interior Ministry tally.

The ministry said 53 police officers and other security personnel were injured, firefighters responded to 75 fires and 24 businesses were looted. It did not provide a figure for injured protesters.

Clashes broke out in cities including Paris, Marseille, Bordeaux, Toulouse and Tours, with police using tear gas. The ministry blamed groups including radical far-left activists for confrontations along or near protest routes.

Authorities counted 410,000 participants at 289 demonstrations and gatherings, down from 550,000 a week earlier. Other estimates put Saturday's turnout as high as 800,000.

The reported arrests and violence were substantially lower than on October 17, when authorities recorded 2,628 detentions and 1,144 injured security personnel.

The ministry said several investigations had been opened and described the subsequent night as calm.

With Saturday's protest over, French Interior Minister Laurent Nunez reported nearly 1,000 arrests.

The number is expected to rise as security officials tally up the day's events, Nunez added.

"Multiple lootings have been averted," he said.

While some clashes between protesters and police were reported in many French cities, the scope of the unrest was much smaller compared to the protests last Saturday, which saw buildings defaced and burned, and some 1,500 shops looted across the country.

Nunez previously put the number of people arrested last weekend at 2,700.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Some 410,000 people marched across France on Saturday, according to the country's interior ministry, as the country braced for a possible repeat of violence seen last weekend.

In Paris alone, at least 110,000 ‌people turned out to denounce rising ⁠living ⁠costs and growing inequality.

Police were out in force in major French cities. While rallies in Paris and Marseille were mostly peaceful, clashes between protesters and police were reported in towards the evening.

Riot police in Paris fired tear gas and water cannons at some 500 hooded youths before they eventually dispersed. In Marseille, youths blocked roads by setting trash cans on fire. They threw rocks and bottles at security forces.

Clashes were also reported in the cities of Lyon, Bordeaux, Grenoble, Clermont-Ferrand, Lille, Rennes, Nantes and Toulouse.

France is known for its protest culture, where demonstration is considered a bedrock value.

Altercations between the police and demonstrators are by and large part of the French protest ritual.

What we are seeing could even be considered a relatively calm end-of-demonstration situation, Lisa Louis, who is in Paris, said.

Meanwhile, security guards were deployed in Milan on Saturday amid demonstrations against the rising cost of living.

Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni ordered 500 soldiers to be dispatched to the city, in addition to about 5,000 police and other law enforcement personnel there.

But the protests in the large Italian city were less unruly than last weekend, when looters ransacked businesses near the central Duomo area. Around 3,000 people rallied in Milan, with another smaller demonstration taking place in Rome.

As the day went on (and largely toward the end of demonstrations in many cities), clashes occurred between protesters and the authorities during the demonstrations across France.

The French interior ministry said it estimates 410,000 people to have taken part in protest throughout France today.

Demonstrations were set to take place in Paris and Marseille but protesters took to the streets in other cities for rallies like Lyon and Nantes.

There were reports about clashes between protesters and police in several cities, including Paris.

There were protests in the city of Lyon, in the east-central part of France, where police used tear gas to disperse protesters wearing masks.

There are also reports about clashes in Marseille, where police fired tear gas to disperse crowds.

About 550,000 people attended demonstrations throughout the country last weekend.

The mood over the past few days in France felt almost a bit apocalyptic. Business owners told me they were worried more looting might lie ahead, after gangs of individuals had ransacked and destroyed at least 3,000 shops across the country last weekend.

"Some of us are planning to buy weapons to defend ourselves, in case the pillagers come back. One day, there's going to be a tragedy — for sure," Jonathan Siad, owner of a tobacco shop in Paris, said to me.

Other store owners were too afraid to talk to us in front of the camera – they worried criminals might see the interview and target their shop.

Residents told me they would try to avoid the area where the demonstration was taking place. Only to add that they hoped the situation wouldn't escalate like last week, when even places far away from the actual marches became hotspots of clashes between the police and protesters.

Interior Minister Laurent Nunez had admitted on Monday a "loss of control" last Saturday. The authorities thus increased the number of police deployed across France by 10,000 to 70,000 this Saturday.

First clashes between protesters and the police started to occur in the late afternoon in several cities across France such as Marseille, Lyon and Paris.

But for now, the much feared mass looting and ransacking doesn't seem to be happening. The situation is nowhere near the total chaos Paris and other cities like Marseille experienced last Saturday.

"Last weekend, our neighborhood resembled a war zone with gangs roaming the area. Today, it's completely empty apart from police with their vans," Yassaman Metghalchi, co-owner of a jewelry shop in the city center of Paris that got looted last Saturday, told me.

"All of our neighbors feel reassured – although the night is still young and we need to wait and see what will happen," she added.

Paris police say 110,000 protesters were on the streets in the city on Saturday. The anti-government "Yellow Vest" movementteamed up with aggrieved school students for this round of demonstrations.

The CGT trade union, meanwhile, said 800,000 participated in protests across France, with 250,000 people taking part in Paris.

Saturday's protesters were limited by Parisian authorities to the city's southeastern edge, away from the center.

Authorities prepared to help the protesters march safely from the Place d'Italie in the 13th arrondissement on the left bank of the Seine River to Porte de Vincennes which lies further away on the right bank.

A post on X by Paris Police Prefecture showed the Porte d'Italie from the sky ahead of the march.

French news agency AFP reported clashes in Paris after the demonstrations.

The French police used tear gas in response to protests in the southeastern city of Lyon. Use of tear gas was also reported in the cities of Lille, Toulouse and Nantes.

In Orleans, south of Paris, protesters also came out to the streets against a two-day congress held in the city of Marine Le Pen's far-right National Rally party.

According to polls, Le Pen is the frontrunner in France's presidential elections next year.

#250. In the biotech world, you can’t see everything

A reporter’s unsettling takeaway from You Can See Everything.

Earlier this week, I attended a screening of You Can See Everything, the buzzy new documentary about the disgraced former biotech CEO Elizabeth Holmes.

I went in not really knowing what to expect. I’d avoided spoilers (and won’t be giving anything away here, either!). I was wondering what new information this film could reveal, given how familiar many of us are with the Theranos story thanks to years of media coverage, the best-selling book Bad Blood, and the Hulu series The Dropout, starring Amanda Seyfried.

Like many others who have seen the film, I came away feeling unsettled. But not just about Holmes’s fraud and the somewhat disturbing interactions between the subjects. The film reignited a concern I’ve had about covering science for most of my career: Despite our best efforts, we never can actually see everything.

In case you’re rusty on the details, Elizabeth Holmes dropped out of Stanford University in 2003 to run Theranos, a diagnostics company she founded, when she was just 19. She went on to claim that her company had invented a way to run over 100 diagnostic tests on just a few drops of blood.

Holmes raised hundreds of millions of dollars for Theranos, which was valued at $9 billion in 2014. Forbes named her the youngest self-made female billionaire—she was worth $4.5 billion when she was just 30 years old.

Things started to unravel in 2015, when an investigation by the Wall Street Journal raised concerns by former Theranos employees that the tests didn’t work. Some also said that the company’s device had not been tested properly, and that blood samples had been analyzed on machines from other companies instead. MIT Technology Review named it one of the biggest technology failures of 2015.

Forbes had revised Holmes’s worth to $0 by 2016. That year, a damning government report said that the company’s lab was struggling with basic procedures and gave patients results even when they knew they could be wrong.

In 2018, Theranos, Holmes, and former company president Ramesh Balwani were charged with “massive fraud.” In 2022, she was sentenced to a little over 11 years in prison for defrauding investors. She began serving her sentence in May 2023.

It was around a month before this that filmmakers Nathan Fielder, Lance Oppenheim, and their colleagues began documenting Holmes and her partner, Billy Evans, at home with their two young children. I’m not giving anything away when I say it’s an extremely intimate portrayal—at least of how the two adults interact with Fielder, who is also a central character in the film.

As you might know if you’ve seen the trailer, there are plenty of super close-ups. I felt as if I spent most of the film staring straight into the faces of those people. And almost the whole way through, they all seemed to be trying to figure each other out. Holmes is known for her dishonesty, but she invited cameras into her home in an apparent bid to reframe the narrative and position herself as an honest person with an unusual mind. Will anyone believe her?

I couldn’t help feeling some empathy for Holmes, a mother we see preparing to leave her two kids for a lengthy prison sentence. And even though I have long known the facts, Holmes is clearly an effective manipulator. You can see her plant the seeds of doubt in Fielder’s mind. At times, I felt she was convincing me, sitting in my cinema seat. Maybe she really didn’t mean to lie?

But then I remembered how other journalists felt after being taken in by her claims. “I blew it,” Fortune’s Roger Parloff reflected in 2015, a year after profiling Holmes and her company. “I’m pretty sure she lied to me,” STAT’s Matthew Herper wrote recently, having interviewed Holmes in 2015. Even the former employee turned whistleblower Tyler Shultz, who knew the product didn’t work, said that he could “have a five-minute conversation with her and walk away feeling like my work was changing the world,” he said in 2022.

I didn’t cover Theranos in the 2010s. If I’d interviewed Holmes, would I have fallen for it all?

One of the early red flags in the Theranos case was the lack of published, peer-reviewed data. Much of the company’s work was classified as a “trade secret.” Scientists tend to publish their work in journals—a slow process that involves having the work evaluated by often anonymous experts in the field, who provide feedback, highlight questionable conclusions, and often ask for clarification. Peer review is meant to add rigor to the publication process. You should be able to trust published scientific papers.

Scientists aren’t perfect. Some will exaggerate their results or inflate their importance. Some manipulate data to serve a hypothesis. Sometimes these infractions are caught during peer review, but not always. There are plenty of examples of scientific findings that could never be replicated by other labs and ended up collapsing under the weight of real evidence. Many papers ultimately end up with corrections. Some are withdrawn—10,000 research papers were retracted in 2023.

At any rate, there’s been a shift to sharing research in other ways. Today, many scientists opt to publish their work at preprint servers like arXiv, medRxiv, and bioRxiv before they submit them to established journals. Those outlets bypass peer review. So do company press releases, social media posts, and many conference presentations. And I get a lot of my stories from conversations and interviews.

I’ll admit I’ve looked at a scientific finding and thought: A scientist has just said they’ve done that. How can I be sure they really have?

I like to think that I hold myself to high journalistic standards here, as does everyone at MIT Technology Review. I would never publish a story about some new scientific development without doing my own form of peer review, and asking scientists—ideally people I know and trust—what they think of a new claim.

But the film is a stark reminder of how easy it can be to fall for an appealing narrative. While most scientists are, I hope, pretty honest, and Holmes is certainly an outlier, we should all continue to interrogate the claims being fed to us. If you’ve seen the film, let me know what you think!

This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here.

New studies pour cold water on a prominent rejuvenation idea.

Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision?

Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age.

#247. Coca-Cola hikes earnings outlook, even as it sees lackluster demand in North America

Coca-Cola on Tuesday raised its full-year outlook for the third straight quarter, despite lackluster demand in its home market.

For the full year, Coke now expects comparable earnings per share to grow at least 11%, up from its prior forecast of an increase in the range of 9% to 10%. The beverage giant also anticipates that its organic revenue will climb at least 5%, a slight increase from its previous outlook of approximately 5% growth.

CEO Henrique Braun attributed the company's strong results to its "speed and discipline" despite the "uneven environment," he said on the company's earnings conference call.

Looking ahead to 2027, the company expects consumer behavior to remain unchanged, CFO John Murphy said. In recent quarters, Braun and other executives have said North American shoppers are under pressure, stretched thin by inflation and economic uncertainty, and looking for value. But the beverage giant has also said consumers are still buying its drinks, from premium brands like Fairlife to its namesake soda.

Shares of the company fell less than 1% in morning trading on its gloomy forecast for the consumer environment.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street analysts surveyed by LSEG were expecting:

Coke reported fiscal third-quarter net income attributable to shareholders of $4.01 billion, or 93 cents per share, up from $3.7 billion, or 86 cents per share, a year earlier.

Excluding transaction gains and other items, Coke earned 91 cents per share.

Net sales rose 7% to $13.4 billion. Coke's organic revenue, which strips out acquisitions, divestitures and foreign currency, also climbed 7% in the quarter.

For the second straight quarter, the company's unit case volume increased 5%, helped by higher demand outside of North America.

Coke's home market reported flat volume. However, the company's North American business would have reported volume growth if not for the timing of the Fourth of July holiday and the monthslong shortage of Topo Chico, a sparkling water brand Coke owns, Murphy told CNBC.

"The underlying momentum remains quite strong," he said. "Consumer sentiment is largely unchanged in the sense that, wherever you are on the income scale, you're looking for value, and I think our system here has done a good job interpreting that."

In North America, Coke's at-home business is faring better than its away-from-home segment, which includes restaurants, movie theaters and amusement parks. The restaurant industry is still under pressure, and Coke expects that to continue through the rest of the year, Murphy told analysts on the company's earnings conference call.

Globally, Coke's sparkling soft drinks business saw volume increase 5% during the quarter, lifted by growth of Coke, Coke Zero Sugar and Diet Coke.

Diet Coke is having a "resurgence," Murphy told CNBC. Gen Z consumers in the U.S. have nicknamed it a "fridge cigarette," while consumers in India threw Diet Coke parties as shortages tied to the U.S.-Israeli war with Iran have made the drink scarce.

"It's a brand that's almost reinventing itself with the next generation of consumers," he said.

Coke has also gained market share as rival PepsiCo sees its soda business struggle, according to Murphy. Still, some of Coke's soda brands, like Sprite, have "some work to do."

Coke's water, sports, coffee and tea division also saw volume climb by 5%. Increased demand for its water, which includes its premium Smartwater brand, fueled the growth. Demand for its tea and coffee was flat, while sports drinks declined.

The company's juice, value-added dairy and plant-based beverages business reported flat volume, despite growth in brands like Fairlife and Minute Maid.

#245. Grok Says Its AI-Generated Version of ‘The Odyssey’ Is ‘Not Historically Accurate’

You don’t hear the defenders of so-called Western civilization talk much about The Odyssey these days.

Maybe that’s because their rabid denunciations of Christopher Nolan’s film adaptation of Homer’s epic were buried in an avalanche of money. The July blockbuster has grossed nearly $1.8 billion worldwide, silencing the right-wingers who predicted a massive flop due to diverse casting and deviations from the source material.

Perhaps the loudest of these preemptive critics was Elon Musk. The world’s richest man spared no vitriol for what he considered a desecration of the ancient Greek narrative; four days after its release, he vowed to produce a superior version. “Before this year ends,” Musk wrote on X, “Grok Imagine will make a full-length movie of The Odyssey that is historically accurate and true to the art of Homer.”

A little more than three months later—long after the discourse moved on—the public finally has a first glimpse of what we might call the Grodyssey, an Odyssey rendered through prompts fed into xAI’s video and image generation model. Rather than a “full-length movie,” it’s a seven-minute “episode” of an apparently ongoing series, depicting how Odysseus, king of Ithaca, resists a call to war against Troy but is ultimately compelled by an oath of loyalty to sail there with his army.

“Odyssey with Grok Imagine,” Musk announced Friday on X when sharing the clip, produced by AI entertainment company Wonder Studios X. The subdued post received just 18,000 likes, notably less engagement than other recent posts in which Musk cast doubt on the results of Brazil’s elections, complained about pronouns, and disparaged “legacy news.”

Grok’s introductory episode bears many hallmarks of AI slop. The movements of faces don’t match the dialog, which is further undercut by stilted and unnatural delivery. Besides Odysseus, none of the principal characters are introduced by name, and the pacing issues are laughable: The Ithacans arrive on the shores of Troy immediately after leaving their homeland, for example. Even the blue checks on X were harsh in their assessment. “Someone actually watched this and still decided to post it?” wrote one user. “Fire that person.”

But Musk’s stated goal wasn’t to deliver a better-looking or more narratively cohesive version of The Odyssey. He and his culture war comrades dubiously claimed that they were against Nolan’s casting of Black and trans actors, the Americanized English spoken by the characters they played, and various designs of ships and armor in the film because they were “historically inaccurate.” Hence Musk’s guarantee of a “historically accurate” corrective.

So, did Grok Imagine succeed on that front? Asking Grok for a self-evaluation yields an interesting answer. “No,” the chatbot says when queried about whether this Odyssey snippet is historically accurate. “Grok Imagine’s Episode 1 (and the broader promised series) is an adaptation of Homer’s Odyssey, not a historically accurate reconstruction of real events.”

The AI model further notes that the characters have no basis in the historical record, and that the poem is dominated by “myth” and “invention” rather than confirmed events. “Homeric society mixes possible Bronze Age elements (palace-centered kingdoms, certain warrior practices) with later Iron Age features,” it also states. “Many details are anachronistic or literary.”

In laying out the reasons that the episode “does not meet a strict historical standard,” Grok points out that the characters speak modern English with British accents, “not Homeric Greek or any reconstructed Bronze Age language.” Visually, it says, the production reflects “modern cinematic tropes and AI training data,” not “primary archaeological evidence.” (The bot cites the appearance of cavalry in a battle scene as an error when compared with standard reconstructions of the period.) Grok concludes that “the Odyssey itself is not a historical document, and the production makes no serious attempt at archaeological or linguistic fidelity beyond a general ‘ancient Greek epic’ aesthetic.”

Of course, the creative license that comes with adapting a millennia-old legend swarming with gods, witches, magic, and monsters was understood by just about everyone but Musk and a handful of cranks insisting that Nolan had contradicted some fundamentally true account of heroic adventure. Even Grok can tell you that its master’s fit of pique led him to promise the impossible—as he so often does.

#244. Israel election live: polls closing soon after historic turnout in vote set to decide Netanyahu’s future

Israel awaits results after prime minister’s bid to extend voting hours failed

What you need to know about Israel’s election

It’s now past 10pm in Israel, which means polling stations across the country have closed after Israelis turned out in record numbers on a high-stakes election day that will determine the future of Benjamin Netanyahu.

A reminder that Israelis waiting in line at polling stations will still be allowed to cast their vote.

Israeli broadcasters are now set to air a set of exit polls, which will provide first estimates of the results. We’ll bring you all the latest on that as those come in.

Polling stations across Israel are set to close in less than 35 minutes, when news tv stations will release their first exit polls giving preliminary estimates of how many seats each party has won.

The first lot of official results then won’t be released until after around 1am local time on Wednesday. And while the exit polls usually provide a reliable indication of how people have voted, they can also vary.

The current coalition is expected to lose its majority, but we won’t expect clear results quickly as the coalition nature of Israeli government means that even small changes in vote share will change coalition calculations. We therefore may not get fuller results until the next day or the day after.

The final tally is expected by 30 October and official election results are formally published a week after the election, on 4 November.

After ​the final tally is ​in, Israel’s president, Isaac Herzog, ⁠will consult party leaders on whom they want as prime minister and then tap the candidate he believes has the best chance ​of forming a government.

That nominee has up to 42 days to form ​a government.

No ⁠single party in Israel has ever won an outright majority, so governments are formed by a coalition of parties who can get to 61 seats – and negotiations on forming a government often take weeks. Every Knesset seat counts and ⁠small ​parties can have a big impact by becoming kingmakers.

If ​the nominee fails, others can get a chance to try. If no government emerges, another election is called, meaning ​Benjamin Netanyahu and his cabinet would remain in place as a “caretaker” government.

It’s now just gone 9pm in Israel, and nationwide voter turnout as of 8pm was 74.3%, per the Central Elections Committee.

That is not only up from 66.3% at the same time in the 2022 election; that figure surpasses the 70.6% total recorded turnout on election day in 2022.

The governor of New York, home to the world’s largest urban Jewish community outside of Israel, has said that her job would get “a lot easier” if Benjamin Netanyahu loses this election.

In an interview with Politico, Kathy Hochul said:

double quotation markI represent the largest [urban] Jewish population in the world outside of Israel. And I know they’re suffering. A lot of people are anxious about antisemitism, which is sky-high right now …

I went to Israel right after October 7. I saw a lot of pain there. But I actually cautioned the government there, in private conversations, not to overreach.

The world is with you when it comes to the brutal assault by … Hamas on innocent Israeli people. An overreach could change that dynamic, and that’s what’s happened.

We have to have leadership there that understands the security of Israel is paramount - critically important to me, but also the world - but to have a safe Palestinian state as well, which has been talked about my entire life.

I still believe that this can happen. I believe we have to get people of goodwill who can make this happen, and as long as Netanyahu is sitting there, that is not in his interest to do that. It’s just not, and it won’t happen.

Voter turnout as of 6pm local time reached 66.7%, the highest ever by that hour since voting hours were first recorded, the Central Elections Committee said.

That is a 9% increase from the 2022 election at that time and Israel’s highest recorded turnout by 6pm.

The Central Elections Committee decided not to extend voting hours beyond 10pm, rejecting a request submitted by Netanyahu bloc parties, Haaretz is reporting.

In a statement, the committee said it met this evening and decided “the current circumstances do not justify an extension”.

It added that the high turnout “reflects strong public engagement in the democratic process” and “no need to extend voting hours was identified”.

A reminder that, under Israeli law, the current rules do allow for a voter to cast their vote past the polling time, just as long they arrive at a station by 10pm.

As we’ve been reporting, a record number of Israelis cast their ballots in the first half of polling day, voting at polling stations across the country and in the Israeli-occupied West Bank.

By 2pm local time (it is now after 8pm), 44.9% of voters had cast their ballot – the highest turnout since record-keeping began in the 1970s.

It’s been described as an existential moment; a battle for the soul of a nation. With voting under way in Israel today, public opinion is divided on what the future of the country looks like. To his supporters, Benjamin Netanyahu has protected the country in a way no other leader could, taking on Iran, Hamas, Hezbollah and Houthis.

To his critics, Netanyahu is to blame for a catastrophic failure of security, leaving Israel more isolated, traumatised and no safer. Today, his most serious challenge comes from a candidate with little experience in office. Gadi Eisenkot is seen as humble, approachable … and honest. The former chief of staff for the Israeli military is promising a future without corruption. Thirteen months ago, his party didn’t even exist.

But after three years of war and upheaval, it’s clear that the consequences of today’s elections go way beyond Israel’s borders.

In the latest edition of our Today in Focus podcast, Emma Graham-Harrison tells Nosheen Iqbal about the stakes of the election and what it could all mean for the future of Israel – and for the Palestinians living under occupation.

In the Palestinian Christian town of Taybeh, Ahmed Tibi, the leader of another Joint List party, Ta’al, had a shouting match with one of the Israeli right wing party representatives over delays, complaining that a woman had fainted while waiting in line.

At a polling station in a Nazareth school this afternoon, an election worker, Bilal Sha’awan shrugged off the far right’s tactics as “childsplay”.

At 3pm there were no queues and a steady flow of voters. A representative of Itamar Ben-Gvir’s Jewish Power party, a young man dressed in the style of settler “hilltop youth”, dressed casually, with a knitted kippa and long sidelocks, strode past Sha’awan, but gave no comment.

Manar Hassan, an observer from Ra’am, the other significant Palestinian Israeli party, described the turnout as “amazing”.

“It’s never been this strong,” she said, adding that the reasons for the higher level participation were not hard to find.

“We witnessed a catastrophe in Gaza,” Hassan said. “Here we live in fear and anxiety. We need drastic change.”

Another hourly voter turn-out update. As has been well established by now this is the largest turnout on record (since the early 1970s when such things were first measured).

As of 6pm local time, 66.7% of eligible voters had cast a ballot. That’s a 9% increase since the last election in 2022.

We can also expect a surge in the Palestinian Israeli vote – which accounts for about 20% of the population – as they tend to vote after work. We should hear shortly the decision of the Central Electoral Committee on whether they will grant the Netanyahu bloc’s request to extend voting hours past 10pm local time. At the moment, polls are due to close in about 2.5 hours.

Tens of thousands of Israelis who live overseas have flown back to Israel to vote. Around 800,000 eligible Israeli voters live outside the country, over 10 percent of the potential electorate, according to Ha’aretz newspaper, but only diplomats can cast a ballot overseas.

For everyone else, taking part in elections requires investing considerable amounts of time and money to fly back.

Yuval Schwarzfuchs, 26, travelled from Rio de Janeiro via Ethiopia, to vote for the Palestinian-led Joint List. “For me, its about equality for all,” he said. “I did what I had to do.”

He came to the Jerusalem polling station wearing a T-shirt with an image of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, the left-wing President of Brazil, who defied polls to win re-election at the weekend.

That victory gave some hope to opponents of Israel’s right-wing government.

“Netanyahu does have a good campaign but I think he will loose,” said Schwarzfuchs, who was at the polling station with his brother, who also voted for the Joint List.

Up to 67,000 voters may be returning to Israel, according to AID Coalition, a U.S.-based nonprofit focused on strengthening Israel’s democracy, so this constituency could be key in a tight race.

In Israel’s proportional representation system, a few thousand votes can have a significant impact on results and around 40,000 votes is expected to secure a seat in the Knesset.

AID coalition organised a “Fly&Vote” initiative to support Israelis who wanted to return for this election, and their figure is based on surveys and registration data. About half of returnees are flying in from the United States, with others from Europe, Canada, Australia and New Zealand.

The project is bipartisan, and not everyone returning to vote opposes the current government, but anecdotal evidence suggests many are opposition supporters who share the view that Israel faces an existential choice.

Monica Moria, 58, and her husband Ron Moria flew in to Tel Aviv from Barcelona. Then they took a train to the kibbutz Kabri in northern Israel, where she is registered to vote, where Moria cast her ballot for the most left-wing of the Jewish Israeli parties, the Democrats.

There were at least five other people at the kibbutz who had flown in to vote, including one who came from Australia and another from the United States.

double quotation markIt feels like Israel has lost its way, there are deep divisions and hatreds that will be very difficult to heal. The idea is to bring a political change so that we can bring Israeli society together again. I want to be optimistic, but I am also afraid.

Kobi Mizrahi, 35, who’s been completing a PhD in New York said he had paid more than $1,600 to come back to Israel for just a few days.

double quotation markGiven the fact that it feels like a “now or never” moment, where if we lose these elections to the Bibi coalition, this might be the turning point we can’t come back to. I felt like I have to do anything in my power to help.

He said the Netanyahu government had abandoned their basic responsibilities, in security, education, transport. “The government isn’t working for us [but] only to maintain political power”.

double quotation markUnfortunately in the past years things have become worse and worse, and in particular after October 7th, when we needed the government the most. Continuing in this direction may bring us, in every aspect, to a point of no return. This is true politically (with extreme parties gaining more power), economically, and morally. The goals of politicians like Betzalel Smotrich to expand settlements in the West Bank hurt all other Israelis in every aspect of our life.

Amid the record turnout, other parties in the Netanyahu coalition camp have joined his Likud party’s request for voting hours to be extended. At the moment, polls are due to close in just under three hours, by 10pm local time. Shas, led by Aryeh Deri, and Amcha Yisrael, led by Ofer Winter, have requested one-hour extensions until 11pm.

Shas said it had requested the extension to “prevent a situation in which citizens waiting in line would be unable to vote.”

However the current rules do allow for a voter to cast their vote past the polling time, just as long they arrive at a station by 10pm.

Most polls show Netanyahu’s Likud and his current coalition parters losing their majority. That fall in voter support has been fairly consistent for more than two years.

They also show a clear anti-Netanyahu majority.

However, those opposition seats are divided between the bloc of Jewish Israeli parties, led by Eisenkot’s Yashar, and parties representing Palestinian citizens of Israel.

The Jewish Israeli parties are unlikely to have a majority on their own, but have mostly ruled out going into coalition with Palestinians.

Palestinian politicians have indicated they might support a minority government of the Jewish Israeli parties in order to unseat Netanyahu’s far-right coalition.

While Jewish Israeli and Palestinian Israeli parties teaming up to form a coalition is seen as a bit taboo – particularly after anti-Arab sentiment rose in the wake of the October 7 attacks – it is not unprecedented. The Ra’am leader, Mansour Abbas, in 2021 took his party into a historic coalition government with Naftali Bennett, now part of the opposition bloc, and Netanyahu himself had previously started coalition negotiations with Abbas. Polling from Channel 12 on the weekend showed too that among the ‘Change’ bloc – what Eisenkot, Bennett, Galan and Liberman are calling their alliance – 60% of voters would prefer a minority government formed with the support of Ra’am from the outside, compared to 20% who would favour repeat elections, and 10% who would want the opposition to unite with Netanyahu.

Here’s a round-up of the some of the party leaders and politicians casting their votes today. If I’m missing a few that’s because I haven’t seen pictures of them voting yet. Netanyahu bloc

His Likud party said in the past hour they’d requested an extension to voting hours from electoral authorities.

He and far-right allies have been running a racist scare campaign today, urging their base to show up to a vote which they claimed was being “stolen”.

The main opposition party leading in the polls, Yashar, has come out saying it “firmly opposes” any extension to voting hours. Polls are open for roughly another 3.5 hours, until 10pm local time.

Gadi Eisenkot’s party called it an attempt to “lay the groundwork for allegations challenging the integrity of the election”.

The Central Election Committee (CEC) – the country’s electoral authorities – is meeting in an hour to consider the request. There have also been several complaints filed by political parties in the anti-Netanyahu bloc to the committee today.

Yashar said it had contacted the CEC following reports some of its ballot slips had been removed or damaged at polling stations

Naftali Bennett’s Together ( B’Yachad) party said it had received reports that an app was being used to monitor voters at polling stations in northern Israel

The Civil Election Guard, a volunteer force of election monitors, said ballot slips for the Haredi Public party in ultra-Orthodox communities had been removed from voting booths or hidden underneath other parties’ papers, according to Haaretz reporting.

Israelis are turning out in record high numbers to vote in an election that many describe as critical. The urgency among opposition voters is even higher than supporters of Netanyahu and his coalition, analysts say.

One of the voters who considers this an existential moment for Israel is Nir Honi, who changed his name by deed poll and has spent eight days inside a small stone circle outside the president’s residence, dressed in biblical- style clothing, in a one-man call for change.

His protest harks back to the story of a first-century Jewish scholar named Honi who convinced God to end a drought with a similar protest.

“(Honi) drew a circle around himself with a stick, and said ‘dear God, I will not move from here until you have mercy on your children and bring us rain’,” the newly renamed Nir Honi said. “My problem is not rain, its a more spiritual issue.”

“We have been in a continuous disaster since October 7th 2023,” he said.

double quotation markWe did not learn anything, we did not investigate, the same government continued and they want to continue for four more years.”

So eight days ago the long-term activist changed his last name to Nir Honi and launched his protest. He has left only once, to vote.

double quotation markOn Monday, which is called the day of the prayer for rain, I came here, I drew a circle in front of the residence of the president of Israel and I said, ‘Dear God, I will not move from here until the October 7th government moves’. From that moment on I have lived in this small circle.”

The Palestinian Israeli vote is a critical camp in this election, representing about one fifth of all eligible voters.

Their votes are expected to go to either Ra’am, led by Mansour Abbas, or Joint List, an electoral alliance of Palestinian-Israeli parties.

But for them them, change will not be straightforward, as my colleague Julian Borger in Nazareth writes.

Even if the opposition bloc led by Gadi Eisenkot wins more seats than Netanyahu’s coalition, it is not expected to win an outright majority on its own, and it has so far ruled out going into coalition with the Palestinian Israeli parties, who could well hold the balance of power ones the votes are counted.

Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, the leading party in the Joint List coalition, thought that the Eisenkot’s approach might change after the elections.

“He cannot sit down [in coalition] with Netanyahu, and he won’t want to go back to another round of elections, so we think he can be flexible,” he said.

In Nazareth, the largest Palestinian-majority town in Israel, the turnout at polling stations has been noticeably stronger than last elections. By 2pm the turnout was over 32%, up from 28% at this time four years ago. That was still below the average Jewish turnout across the country, but a surge of evening voting is expected here as voters come home from work. At the headquarters of the Joint List, the biggest coalition of Palestinian-Israeli parties, the most pressing concern was the spoiler tactics of far-right election monitors at the predominantly Palestinian polling stations. “They can say that they want to check all the voter numbers and identity and everything, so they can slow things down, so that people wait a long time and give up. That’s their intention,” Amjad Shbita, the general secretary of Hadash, a socialist movement which is the leading party in the Joint List coalition. At the worst, these tactical ploys have slowed the process down so that it took 30 minutes to vote, rather than just a couple, he said. The Joint List sent its workers out to the polling station to provide water and folding chairs so that voters would not lose patience and go home. Shbita said that Palestinian Israel motivation is strong enough to overcome such obstacles. “It is the first time for a long time that a government has stayed in office for its full term, four years,” he said. “We are talking about one of the worst governments when it comes to Arab society. We feel racism at all levels of Israeli society, in hospitals, in buses, in public transportation, in the media. So people understand that today we have the opportunity to change the government.”
