For your boss, the cart includes a vegan Freshroll fresh spring roll filled with sesame-lime tofu and served with Grandma's peanut sauce ($5.85). For you, there's a regular bánh mì sandwich loaded with garlic-, ginger-, and honey-roasted pork ($10.85), paired with a Vietnamese cold-brew iced coffee ($5.85) for an afternoon caffeine boost—bringing the pickup subtotal to $22.55.

Another major selling point of these tools is their ability to be proactive, compared to chatbots that essentially only react to your prompts. Before I started the test, I created a Google Calendar event for 2 pm, labeling it as an important source meeting that can’t be moved. I also sent an email from my work account to my personal account, with the made-up scenario that my mom was getting out of the hospital today, and I needed to call her at 2 pm to send love and support from afar.

Which AI agents would read the email and calendar event, identify the conflict, and message me before the overlap to suggest alternatives? Only two of the bots succeeded here, which was fewer than I expected.

A possible clash this afternoon: your note to call Mom says 2 pm, and your important source meeting is 2-3 pm, Pacific. Would calling her before the meeting or after 3 work with her discharge timing?

Reece, good morning. Your 2 pm source meeting runs until 3, right when your own reminder says to call your mom.

I should have caught it, and from now on I'll check your self-reminder emails along with your calendars.

I had nothing cross-checking your inbox against your calendar. Want me to run that check every morning and flag collisions before they hit?

My mistake was treating your calendar as the only source — I never cross-checked that email reminder against it, even though Gmail was right there.

Because the reminder arrived as an email message rather than a calendar entry or scheduled task, the cross-app overlap wasn't surfaced before 2:00 pm.

I rarely go on vacation, so when I do, I spend an obscene number of hours researching, and I relish it. I love watching YouTube street food tours and aimlessly scrolling through flights trying to find the best deal. So I don't want to automate this task fully. Even so, I’m open to the idea that these AI agents could help with some of the logistical planning.

As with any AI-generated output, I still recommend checking multiple, non-AI sources before trusting these itineraries or revolving your whole trip around what agents recommend. When travelers in California recently trusted a chatbot to plan everything for a strenuous hiking excursion, the group had to be rescued because they lacked critical supplies (and maybe some critical thinking).

For this test, I sent all the AI agents a long paragraph explaining that my partner and I are considering exploring China late next year during a two-week-long adventure. I asked for a first draft of a potential itinerary that included: flight, hotel, nightlife, and food recommendations. Also, I asked the AI agents to chart a course that blends historical moments with big-city vibes. I can’t stand constantly changing locations, so I told the agents I want to stay in four different hotels max.

Notably, all of them chose identical routes through China—potentially ushering a new era of the Lonely Planet effect, where groups of travelers are nudged to visit similar locations, shops, and restaurants.

Fly out of SFO the week after Golden Week, on a nonstop to Beijing, home nonstop from Shanghai. Four hotels, no backtracking — imperial history first, Sichuan food and pandas in the middle, Shanghai nightlife to finish.

AI agent snippet: My recommendation: fly into Beijing, take high-speed trains to Xi’an and Chengdu, then fly to Shanghai and home. You get imperial history, the Terracotta Army, Sichuan food and a big-city finale without an overnight train or an extra hotel move.

A first-draft 13-night trip for two: Beijing, Xi'an, Chengdu and Shanghai, departing SFO on Oct 13 and returning Oct 27, after Golden Week.

A First-Time Traveler's Route Blending Imperial History, Sichuan Gastronomy, Vibrant Nightlife & 4-Hotel Hub Comfort.

Beijing in, Shanghai home, with Xi'an and Chengdu between: four bases, no backtracking, history up front and the food and bars getting better every stop.

Fly into Beijing on Saturday, Oct 9, 2027, then go Beijing → Xi'an → Chengdu → Shanghai, and fly home from Shanghai on Saturday, Oct 23. That's 13 nights in four hotels, two bullet trains and one short flight.

I was having trouble sleeping after all this AI agent chaos, though the Vietnamese coffee probably didn’t help. What happens when you give these tools a broad, open-ended task? Sometimes when I need the most help is when I don’t even know where to start.

If I had a human assistant, I would never message them in the middle of the night, so maybe that’s a plus for these digital helpers. The two agents that really flopped at this task, Gemini Spark and Claude Duet, responded to me without the soft touch or situational context a real person might.

I can set a reminder for later this morning to check in on your mom and see how her leg's doing, so it's off your mind for now.

Send me the three things taking up space in your head; messy is fine, and I’ll turn them into a short checklist with one clear first step. Then you can put the phone down.

Here's one you can do from bed: skim the China draft and tell me the one thing you'd change. I'll have a v2 ready when you wake up.

I'll cross-check today's calendar and your self-sent email reminders for any conflicts or can't-miss items—the check I should've done properly yesterday—and only surface it at a sane hour, unless something's urgent early.

The best quick task right now is a mental download to get whatever is looping in your head onto paper so your brain can let go of it—especially since your calendar is completely clear today, with no morning commitments waiting for you.

Let's close out yesterday's two loose ends. Your Thursday pitch is delivered, and your lunch cart got covered by the DragonEats order. Say the word, and I'll mark both done, then you can get back to sleep.

Yes, it’s still behind an expensive paywall, so I would suggest most people wait until OpenAI launches this agent for free, or at least on the $20-a-month plan, before trying it out. Even so, Dots performed the best overall in these tests and failed at a task that’s fairly specific to my niche.

In the month since its release, Dots already feels more polished. I prefer a separate app over the current ChatGPT integration, though the smartphone widget option is a decent compromise. Dots for the win.

Muse is for the normies! And that’s fine. It did well in tests, coming in a close second on the scorecard, and was the easiest to set up. Oh, and it's free. Despite Meta’s assurances that it responsibly processes user info in secure virtual machines, I still have concerns given the company’s past mishandling of user data.

I tested this AI agent just one day after it officially launched as a research preview, so a few rough edges were expected. Despite Claude Duet scoring in the middle of the pack on most tests, its dedicated app was extremely user-friendly to navigate and follow up on ongoing tasks. I could see users obsessed with productivity and streamlined software experiences really latching onto Claude Duet in the next couple of months. Though you currently have to sign up for Anthropic’s waiting list to access Duet, and it’s behind a $20-a-month paywall.

You might like the sparse design fully integrated into your messenger; I did not. While other WIRED writers have lauded Instinct, calling it so good that they didn’t care what happened to their privacy, the texting-only aspect felt like a deal-breaker for me. I left the experience feeling too removed from what my agent was doing on my behalf and annoyed at its emoji reactions, like a grimace face when I told the agent it missed my scheduling conflict.

#235. Melting glaciers in Dolomites reveal cache of first world war munitions

Italian army removes and defuses 250 explosives encased in ice for more than 100 years after hikers’ sightings

A significant cache of unexploded bombs from the first world war emerged this summer from rapidly retreating glaciers in the Dolomites, the Italian army has said.

The devices, which included Italian artillery shells and French mortar bombs, were removed and defused by the army’s Julia Alpine brigade, according to a statement.

The weapons originated from the white war, a battle between the Austro-Hungarian army and Italian troops across treacherous and bitterly cold terrain between 1915 and 1918.

More than a century later, hikers reported several sightings of bombs during the summer, mostly in the area of Canal San Bovo in the Primiero Valley in eastern Trentino. The soldiers then conducted an extensive sweep there and across other high-altitude and glacier locations in the Trentino Alto Adige region of the Dolomites.

They recovered and neutralised 250 explosives, including “18 Italian 75mm artillery shells and 37 Thevetot mortar shells – a French-made device that was also supplied to the Italian royal army during the first world war”, the Julia Alpine brigade.

It is not the first time that relics of the white war have emerged from melting glaciers, whose retreat has accelerated over more than decade.

“It is very much a consequence of global heating,” said Stefano Morosini, a professor at the University of Bergamo and the coordinator of heritage projects at Stelvio national park, where some of the explosives were also found.

“In recent years, at the end of each summer, these dangerous artefacts emerge and each year there is a higher number. They have been conserved by the ice for more than 100 years. Some had already exploded but many hadn’t, so now when they re-emerge, they are very, very dangerous.”

The advice to hikers and mountaineers is to “never touch any type of explosive device you might find, regardless of where it is located” and to promptly report any discovery to police.

Fought mainly in the Dolomites of Trentino Alto-Adige and the Alps of the Lombardy region, the white war was a period of history frozen in time until the 1990s, when global heating started to reveal an assortment of perfectly preserved relics, including weapons, sledges, letters and diaries.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

A wooden barracks that had been dug into a cave on the top of Mount Scorluzzo, a 3,095-metre (10,154ft) peak overlooking the Stelvio pass, reappeared in 2015 after the ice which had sealed it off for almost a century completely melted.

The shelter had been hastily locked up when the first world war ended in November 1918, and the soldiers abandoned most of their belongings. Beds made of straw as well as clothes, lanterns, newspapers, postcards, coins, and tinned food were found well preserved inside.

A white war museum to host the relics is scheduled to open in Bormio in June 2027.

The white war is thought to have claimed more than a million lives, although the majority of soldiers were believed to have been killed by avalanches, falling down mountains or hypothermia.

Dozens of corpses, some still in their uniforms, have emerged from the melting ice over the past 15 years.

The relatives of an Italian soldier were able to bury him in 2017 after documents revealing his identity were found with his body on Presena glacier.

Morosini said the intense summer heat was making access to the glaciers in the Dolomites less safe. Eleven people died when a huge mass of the Marmolada glacier broke off in 2022.

“It is becoming more and more impossible to access the glaciers during the summer because of the very long heatwave at high altitude,” he said.

“If you come to the area, you can see how fast the glaciers are retreating and how strong the impact of climate change is.”

#230. T-Mobile’s new T-Mix plans are announced: Are the streaming perks worth the switch?

TL;DR: T-Mobile is overhauling its unlimited plans with T-Mix — starting at $55 a month with AutoPay — trading traditional bundled perks for customizable "Picks" and slightly lower single-line pricing.

T-Mobile is shuffling its lineup once again. After nudging rates upward over the past couple of years, the carrier is introducing T-Mix, a trio of plans built around an à la carte menu of digital freebies.

Instead of baking the same streaming bundle into everyone's account, T-Mobile gives you a monthly allotment of Picks that you can redeem for entertainment apps, food delivery perks, airline miles, or connectivity upgrades.

The lineup lands in November and comes in three tiers (all assuming AutoPay with a debit card, bank account, or T-Mobile Visa, with taxes and government fees billed separately):

T-Mix Standard ($55/month for one line) — Comes with 50GB of premium data, 480p standard-definition streaming, zero high-speed mobile hotspot (you're throttled to basic 3G speeds), and 1 Pick.

T-Mix More ($75/month for one line) — Bumps you to unlimited premium data, 4K streaming, 60GB of high-speed mobile hotspot, solid international roaming (15GB in Canada/Mexico, 5GB abroad), and 2 Picks.

T-Mix Loaded ($95/month for one line) — Delivers unlimited premium data, 4K streaming, a massive 250GB mobile hotspot allowance, hefty roaming data (30GB in Canada/Mexico, 15GB abroad), and 3 Picks.

Adding extra lines also grows your pool of Picks. Lines two and three add the same allotment as your base plan, while a fourth line adds one extra Pick on Standard and More, and two on Loaded. Discounted versions will also be available for seniors (55+), military and veterans, first responders, and college students.

T-Mobile gives you a set monthly allowance of credits to spend on a menu of add-ons. Basic services cost one Pick, while pricier ad-free plans and gaming passes cost more:

1-Pick freebies — Ad-supported Netflix, ad-supported Hulu, Apple TV (for a limited promotional window), Paramount+ Essential, DoorDash DashPass, Google AI Plus, Pandora Plus, SiriusXM app access, YouTube Premium Lite, 500 Delta SkyMiles each month, or T-Satellite roaming (via Starlink).

2-Pick freebies — Ad-free YouTube Premium, Apple Music Individual, Google AI Pro, Pandora Premium, or Xbox Game Pass Premium.

3- to 4-Pick freebies — Higher-tier upgrades like Netflix Standard (ad-free) or Xbox Game Pass Ultimate for 3 Picks, and ESPN Unlimited or Netflix Premium for 4 Picks.

You are not stuck with your choices forever, either. You can swap your partner perks four times a year — during three dedicated change windows plus an “Annual Remix” window — while network-level add-ons like T-Satellite can be toggled whenever you want.

All tiers still include standard weekly discount drops through T-Mobile Tuesdays. You can also bank one to three Picks per line to unlock discounted pricing or trade-in deals when upgrading your phone.

It depends on how many lines you need and how willing you are to strategize over subscription vouchers. If you only need one or two lines, T-Mix More and Loaded can actually come in lower than previous Experience and Go5G equivalents. If you already pay cash out of pocket for perks like DashPass or YouTube Premium, the bundled value adds up quickly.

Families, however, have good reason to pause. Because older setups frequently leaned on third-line-free promotions, accounts with three or four lines could easily pay more under T-Mix than on legacy plans.

Don't forget about extra fees, either. Unlike classic Magenta and Go5G setups that rolled regulatory surcharges and taxes straight into the advertised rate, T-Mix bills them on top, so your final credit card charge will inevitably run several dollars higher than the sticker price.

If you are happy with what you are paying and already use the freebies tied to your account, the smartest move right now is to do absolutely nothing.

Soumya is a deals writer who covers consumer tech, shopping deals, and the products people use every day. With experience writing about everything from AI tools and software to smartphones and home gadgets, she enjoys breaking down product research into clear, useful recommendations. When she's not tracking deals, she's usually comparing products, digging through reviews, and figuring out what actually makes a purchase worth it.

#229. Steinmeier in China: A major state visit in uncertain times

President Frank-Walter Steinmeier is traveling to China on a state visit in a period of political and economic uncertainty. Germany is seeking alternative partners in the region but also wants good ties with Beijing.

Eight years have passed since Frank-Walter Steinmeier's last trip to China. Since then, the relationship between China and Europe has changed significantly. The German president plans to spend several days touring China before concluding his visit in Beijing, where he will meet with China's leaders.

His visit comes just as the Chinese Communist Party's Central Committee is convening for its plenary session, a major annual event that foreign dignitaries usually avoid when timing their visits.

The focus of the president's five-day trip will be "Germany's diverse and complex relations with China, as well as the challenges involved," his office said in a statement. In addition to sites of cultural exchange, the itinerary will also include a visit to a Volkswagen plant in Hefei.

The automotive industry, in particular, illustrates the dilemma facing Germany's China policy. China is one of the most important markets for German automotive and machinery manufacturers, as well as for the chemical industry. At the same time, state-supported overcapacity in China is putting pressure on European manufacturers.

Not only that. Germany and Europe are dependent on China for raw materials and technologies, which means they are increasingly seeking out alternative partnerships.

Steinmeier was once an advocate of the policy of "change through trade" — the idea that other countries could be persuaded to become more democratic with the leverage of global trade — which has few adherents left in Germany today.

Now he must now pursue a tougher European line in Beijing. Caught between partnership and competition, Europe leaders recently managed to reach an agreement: Imports of plug-in hybrid vehicles from China are to be scaled back.

"That was a concrete step, but it can only be the beginning," said Michael Laha, an expert on China at the German Council on Foreign Relations (DGAP) in Berlin.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Coinciding with Steinmeier's state visit to China, Chancellor Friedrich Merz is traveling to the Indo-Pacific with business representatives. His visit to Japan, Singapore and South Korea will focus on competitiveness, technological upheavals, defense cooperation and critical raw materials. Merz is visiting companies and attending important regional economic conferences in Tokyo and Seoul.

The dual trips are Berlin's attempt to send a twofold message: While the chancellor is "deliberately strengthening Germany's partnerships in the Indo-Pacific at a time of global upheaval," according to Laha, the president is seeking dialogue with Beijing at a time when Germany and Europe are recalibrating their relationship with China.

"This joint trip sends a very well-coordinated message to the Chinese that we remain committed to the partnership but at the same time are taking steps to reduce risk," said Laha, before adding that this is a difficult balancing act that also allows China to save face.

While Merz will be accompanied by a large economic delegation, Steinmeier will be traveling to China with representatives from German culture, politics, and academia. Mikko Huotari, director of the China Institute MERICS, is also expected to be part of the delegation — even though Beijing has imposed sanctions on the institute. "The fact that he is coming along sends a clear message to the Chinese side that we do not approve of the sanctions," said Laha.

In 2021, the EU imposed sanctions against several Chinese party and regional officials, as well as an organization accused of serious human rights violations and oppressing the Uyghur minority. It was the first time such sanctions had been imposed on China since the Tiananmen Square massacre in 1989, and Beijing did not take kindly to them. In response, the Chinese government imposed entry bans and other sanctions against European politicians, scholars, and institutions, including MERICS.

Laha did not expect human rights to play a major role in the German president's trip. Steinmeier is considered a pragmatist when it comes to China policy. As foreign minister, he distanced himself from his predecessor Joschka Fischer, a critic of China, and even criticized Chancellor Angela Merkel when she hosted the Dalai Lama.

On the subject of Ukraine, Steinmeier is likely to be more direct and raise the issue of China's role in the conflict. "Beijing is clearly on Moscow's side," said Laha. In Berlin, however, officials are convinced that China can do more to persuade Russia to engage in serious negotiations and find a solution to the conflict.

Steinmeier may make this clear in his speech at Peking University on Thursday. Germany has "long viewed China's emergence with respect and goodwill," read a statement from the president's office. Today, however, the focus is less on China's continued rise and more on how it uses its growing power.

"If the president points out that Russia used its power to attack a sovereign state, he could be alluding to another regional connection: Taiwan," said Laha. That could be seen as an appeal to Beijing to assume more responsibility for the regional and international order.

To conclude his visit, Steinmeier will meet with China's leadership in Beijing. Talks are planned with President Xi Jinping, whom he has known for nearly two decades, as well as with Premier Li Qiang and the chairman of the National People's Congress, Zhao Leji.

The trip marks the culmination of Steinmeier's focus on Asia during his second term in office. Since then, he has deliberately sought to forge close ties with partners in the region and has advocated for greater diversification. Now China, which is Germany's most important and most challenging partner, will come to its own conclusion.

#228. A 'canary in the coal mine': Measles resurgence exposes weaknesses in U.S. public health, experts say

For nearly two years, the U.S. has had to swallow an uncomfortable reality: A highly infectious disease it once eliminated is spreading again.

More than 4,400 measles cases and at least three deaths have been reported nationwide this year, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. The spike followed nearly 2,300 cases in 2025. But many public health experts argue that the return of measles isn't the only concerning development — it's also what the resurgence signals about America that's causing alarm.

Measles is often described as one of the most contagious viruses, and it is uniquely unforgiving of weaknesses in public health defenses. A safe and highly effective vaccine has existed for decades.

But the virus's spread exposes declining vaccination rates, eroding trust in health institutions and growing challenges in persuading people to protect themselves, their children and their communities, experts said.

Together, those vulnerabilities have made measles a revealing test of the country's public health defenses. For some experts, the resurgence of a disease the U.S. eliminated more than two decades ago is a warning sign of its ability to contain future threats, from other vaccine-preventable diseases to emerging pathogens.

"Measles is the canary in the coal mine for so many different reasons," said Jennifer Nuzzo, an epidemiology professor and director of the Pandemic Center at Brown University School of Public Health. "It's the canary in the coal mine for other vaccine-preventable illnesses, where we could see a return of things that used to harm kids, but also for very acute, no-notice dangerous infectious disease emergencies."

The U.S. is on track to lose its official measles elimination status during an upcoming review by the Pan American Health Organization, which will meet in Washington, D.C., in mid-November.

"It says a lot about a country if it is unable to control measles," said Nuzzo, who is one of 10 experts CNBC spoke with about the ongoing outbreak and its implications.

The U.S. has the tools to prevent measles from spreading. But pockets of low vaccination coverage are leaving the country vulnerable.

The measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine is about 97% effective against measles after two doses, while a single dose is 93% effective, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Yet data aggregated by the CDC shows that vaccination coverage for that shot among U.S. kindergarteners fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, which is below the 95% threshold public health officials generally recommend to prevent sustained transmission.

That translates to 280,000 kindergarteners who did not complete their MMR vaccination series, according to the CDC. Childhood vaccination exemptions in the U.S. also reached a record high 4.2% for incoming kindergartners during the 2025-2026 school year, up from 3.6% the previous year, the agency says.

"The bottom line is that not enough people are vaccinating their kids," said Brown's Nuzzo.

While some cases stem from international travel, much of the recent spread has been driven by local transmission in "too many" communities where vaccination coverage falls below 95%, she added.

Among states across the U.S. collecting MMR vaccine data, 67% of counties and jurisdictions now have immunization rates below 95%, according to a September investigation by NBC News.

Recent U.S. outbreaks have often occurred in "closely knit communities" where people have frequent contact and low vaccination coverage, said Dr. William Moss, an epidemiology professor at Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health.

But declining vaccination rates do not mean most Americans have turned against immunization. Polling suggests broad support for childhood vaccines. A July KFF survey found that many Americans sit in a more uncertain middle ground about the shots, leaving room for conflicting information to influence their decisions.

"This is not an edge-case issue," Nuzzo said, adding that the vast majority of parents still vaccinate their children.

Even if outbreaks are concentrated in a minority of communities, it can create a "dangerous situation" far from those areas, she said. Infants too young to be vaccinated and people who cannot receive immunizations for medical reasons can be particularly vulnerable when the virus spreads into communities with high vaccination rates.

Those risks come as the Trump administration reshapes federal vaccine policy.

President Donald Trump signed an executive order in August suggesting that the combined MMR vaccine be split into separate shots, while repeating false claims about its safety. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has also overhauled a key federal advisory committee responsible for recommending childhood immunizations, though the changes are frozen amid ongoing litigation.

Several experts told CNBC that such moves risk further eroding confidence in vaccines and weakening efforts to protect children.

Public health infrastructure was also "really decimated by Covid," and the CDC in particular lost many experienced scientists and physicians "who had spent their lives working on vaccine-preventable diseases," said Scott Weaver, a virologist who is the research director of Galveston National Laboratory.

He added that the CDC, as of September, is also no longer immediately incorporating state-reported "measles-associated" deaths into its national published tally, making it harder to track outbreaks in real time. The agency lists three measles-related deaths in 2026, but Pennsylvania has reported at least five deaths.

"We have folks in federal health leadership right now that are throwing gasoline on the fire, and that is not helping the situation," said Jess Steier, a public health scientist and founder and CEO of Unbiased Science, an organization that aims to make complex scientific concepts understandable.

If the U.S. fails to materially improve vaccination rates, the measles resurgence could extend beyond ongoing outbreaks.

At "current" state-level vaccination rates, measles could become endemic again in the U.S., resulting in an estimated 851,300 cases over 25 years, according to a large-scale epidemiological model published in JAMA in April 2025 by researchers at Stanford and other institutions. Under that scenario, the model predicts the measles cases could lead to 170,200 hospitalizations and 2,550 deaths over that period.

The model, which simulated infectious disease spread in the U.S. at various childhood vaccination levels, relies on immunization data from 2004 to 2023 for that baseline scenario. The model assumes that diseases are introduced by someone who travels abroad and brings them back, most commonly a U.S. citizen who isn't vaccinated.

While many people are currently immune through vaccination, outbreaks can increase in size and frequency if immunization rates decline over a longer period.

Michael Osterholm, an epidemiologist and the director of the Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy at the University of Minnesota, said he views the measles situation in Canada as a direct warning for the U.S.

Canada lost its official measles elimination status in November after a multi-province outbreak that generated more than 5,400 cases across 10 jurisdictions in 2025. Canada has a population of more than 40 million people.

"We have a population of 350 million. If you extrapolate, if we had the very same experience that Canada had over the next year or two, that would be up to 50,000 cases of measles in the U.S.," Osterholm said. "I think that's a potential future we have to be mindful of."

An even larger measles outbreak isn't the only potential problem.

Vaccination coverage for other childhood diseases has similarly declined. The share of U.S. kindergartners vaccinated against polio fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, while coverage for diphtheria, tetanus and acellular pertussis, or DTaP, dropped to 92%, according to CDC data.

The U.S. has already seen a sharp rise in pertussis, or whooping cough. U.S. reported cases reached roughly 43,300 in 2024 and nearly 28,000 in 2025, up substantially from pandemic-era levels, according to the CDC. Brown's Nuzzo said the resurgence reflects, in part, fewer people getting vaccinated and the fact that protection from the pertussis vaccine wanes over time.

The simulation model published in JAMA also examined how falling childhood immunization rates could affect the future spread of several diseases previously eliminated from the U.S. through vaccines. Under current vaccination levels, the researchers found that diseases including rubella and polio were unlikely to become endemic in the U.S.

But in a hypothetical scenario in which childhood vaccination rates fell by 50%, the model projected 9.9 million cases of rubella, 4.3 million cases of polio and 197 cases of diphtheria over 25 years.

Rubella can cause devastating complications when an infection during pregnancy passes to a developing fetus, while polio can cause paralysis and death, said Dr. Nathan Lo, assistant professor of infectious diseases at Stanford Medicine.

The projections are not predictions that these outbreaks will occur, but they illustrate how much risks could grow if vaccination coverage deteriorates.

"What vaccines often buy you is not just the protection, but also less uncertainty," said Mathew Kiang, an assistant professor of epidemiology and population health at Stanford Medicine who also worked on the simulation model. "I think even more frightening is just the really wide uncertainty that we just wouldn't be sure of what could happen."

The measles resurgence is also raising questions about how effective the U.S. can be at detecting and responding to emerging public health threats before they spread.

"Public health is all about prevention," said Dr. Manisha Juthani, president of the Association of State and Territorial Health Officials and commissioner of the Connecticut Department of Public Health. She said the country must maintain its ability to detect threats, invest in surveillance, and recognize that infectious diseases do not respect national borders.

Those capabilities are crucial for curbing emerging pathogens, especially when scientists initially know little about how a new disease spreads, how severe it is or how to treat it. A pathogen could spread before vaccines or treatments are available, making early detection, clear public communication and coordination especially important.

Other experts argue that weaknesses in those capabilities could also have implications for the country's defenses against deliberate biological attacks.

In an April 2025 op-ed, Steph Batalis, a research fellow at Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology, argued that the measles outbreak exposed a broader vulnerability in the U.S.' biodefense strategy and, specifically, a weakened ability to deter potential biological attacks.

She argued that the country's struggle to contain a known, vaccine-preventable disease "might only embolden those who see our public health vulnerabilities as an opportunity."

But Osterholm, who works extensively on bioterrorism, said low MMR immunization rates and rising measles cases do not necessarily indicate that Americans would refuse a vaccine or antibiotic during a biological emergency. The shots and drugs used to counter biological threats can differ from those routinely administered to the public, he said, and the prospect of a severe outbreak could prompt urgency among the public to seek protection.

That point highlights an important limitation: The measles resurgence by itself does not establish whether the U.S. could withstand entirely different or novel public health threats.

But as a disease the U.S. knows how to prevent spreads, experts say the stakes of addressing those vulnerabilities extend to whatever might come next.

#227. Is an Under-Sink Reverse Osmosis System Worth It? Benefits, Costs, and Drawbacks

That funny taste in your tap water may be a harmless nuisance—or it may be cause for serious concern. Either way, you can do something about it by installing a reverse osmosis (RO) water filtration system in your home.

Stand-alone RO systems and pitchers sit on your countertop and generally require manual refilling from the tap. You’ll save space and avoid the hassle of constantly refilling the unit by installing an under-sink or under-counter model that connects directly to your plumbing lines. Either way, RO systems aren’t cheap. But for some homeowners, they may be an invaluable investment.

Osmosis is the movement of molecules—usually water—through a semipermeable membrane that blocks larger particles like salts and sugars from passing through. After osmosis, the concentration of particles on both sides of the membrane ends up in equilibrium. It’s how plants get water from the soil, and it’s how your kidneys filter junk out of your blood.

While osmosis is a spontaneous phenomenon that happens naturally, reverse osmosis requires pressure to force water through a membrane in the other direction, effectively removing all of those compounds from the water to render it pure. It’s how salt is removed from seawater to make it drinkable, and it’s one way tap water can be purified in your home.

RO differs considerably from traditional filtration systems, which rely on physical barriers, typically activated carbon, to manually capture particles that pass through. Eventually, these filters become saturated with sediments and other matter and have to be replaced. While carbon filters are fairly blunt, able to capture larger particles of at least 0.1 microns in size, RO systems have a much finer-grained membrane that captures contaminants on a microscopic level (typically down to 0.0001 microns).

Carbon filters can make your water taste and smell better, but with RO you are getting much, much cleaner water than what that inexpensive Brita filter can muster. (That said, RO can’t capture everything, and carbon filters are still useful for capturing certain volatile organic compounds through adsorption. Most RO systems also have an additional carbon filtration system included.)

Note that two types of RO systems are available: Tanked RO systems filter water slowly (albeit passively, without electricity) and sequester it in a (rather large) tank under your sink. Today, tankless RO systems are more popular, processing water on demand but requiring electricity to keep things flowing expeditiously. Tankless systems are considerably more expensive due to their inherent complexity.

“US tap water is safe for most people most of the time,” says John Voll, an industrial water treatment veteran and the founder of Tap & Air, a website that monitors water and air quality for hundreds of municipalities. “The problems are local, not universal.”

The first step to understanding your personal risk is to gauge whether your own water is up to snuff by simply looking up its most recent data report online. The Safe Drinking Water Act requires community water systems to publish an annual report on the quality of water they’re delivering, due by July 1 each year.

These are heady documents, so to keep things simple, Voll says to keep your eye on three key pollutant metrics: lead, nitrate or arsenic, and PFAS (so-called forever chemicals popular in nonstick cookware and other products). While the first two are serious issues, they’re fairly rare today. PFAS, however, are a particularly significant problem across the country. Voll says that 98 of the 200 largest US cities have at least one PFAS compound detected in their water supply. Reverse osmosis systems can handle them—as well as lead and other compounds. Carbon filters usually can’t.

“Most people don't need RO,” Voll says, “but some households clearly do, and the way to tell is to read your own water report first.”

RO systems come with a host of downsides, but most are related in some fashion to cost.

Based on my analysis of my city’s water report, no I don’t really need an RO system, as there’s nothing hazardous to worry about. However, as the linked report notes, our water supplier uses chloramine as a disinfectant, and drinking that chloramine-treated water straight from the tap has a slight but noticeable, off-putting taste. This is exacerbated by the extreme hardness of the water; this part of Texas is basically built on top of nothing but limestone, and the resulting amount of calcium carbonate in the water can add a slightly bitter flavor, as well.

For me, RO is all about the way the water tastes. Any lingering contaminants that are removed along the way are just a bonus.

I tested the Waterdrop X12 for this story, hiring a plumber to remove a well-aged unit under the sink and replace it with this more capable one. The job was done in less than an hour.

Under-sink RO filters are separate from your existing plumbing, and most use three color-coded lengths of tubing to tie into it. One connects to your incoming water supply through an adapter valve, one clips into your wastewater drainpipe via an included fitting, and one connects to the faucet. I am confident I could have made these connections on my own—especially since I was replacing an existing system—but I’ve seen how even a small water leak can lead to devastating problems and was happy for the peace of mind that professional oversight supplied.

Once installed and powered up, the X12 launches a 35-minute priming process designed to flush the filters before first use. It seems like an awfully long time for the sink to run, and it is. If you have some way to capture and use all this extra water, plan ahead to collect it. After the session completed (it stops automatically), I was ready to start sipping from the tap.

The X12 is exceedingly simple to use. Tap the LED-equipped button on top of the faucet to start or stop the flow of water, or twist the same button to dial in the number of ounces you want to dispense (up to 999) before depressing it. This way, you can drop your 64-ounce Stanley Quencher in the sink and walk away without having to wait around or worry about an overflow, with a digital timer first offering a measurement of your water’s current TDS (total dissolved solids), then counting down the ounces as they’re dispensed. The good news is that you won’t have to wait long: The X12 blasts water out of its spigot faster than my kitchen sink does—a far cry from the sad dribble of H2O my old RO system provided.

The unit can also be switched into one of two alternate operating modes, including an energy-saving mode (which quiets the device) and “flow mode,” which is designed for high-use households. Both require pressing certain button combinations on the filtration unit. I have used the device in all three modes, and (contrary to other reviews I’ve seen) I’ve never found it to be noisy at all, even though it’s located just 20 feet from my bed.

As for the water, it tastes great. The remineralization feature and pH adjustment (to about 7.5, which I confirmed with a pH test strip) give the water a pleasant tang while avoiding any harshness or chemical flavors. Unfortunately it doesn’t come out particularly cold, which means having to add non-RO ice to the glass to chill it down. First-world problem, I know.

I was especially pleased to discover that the X12 has one of the best water efficiency rates in the business, with a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio (3 gallons of pure water production to 1 gallon lost). Replacement filters (three are needed in total) are sold à la carte for $30 to $160 each.

For an even more powerful version—ideal for larger households—consider the Waterdrop X16, which uses the same cartridges and offers the same features as the X12. WIRED editor Kat Merck, whose municipal water supply does contain PFAS, has had one installed for a year and a half without any maintenance or reliability issues.

#226. UK officials discussing options including free movement with EU ahead of summit

Business secretary says focus for November summit will be trade and youth mobility, but talks will also include ‘future options for relationship’

British officials have begun discussions with the EU about a long-term relationship that could include a return to free movement, the business secretary has said while promising the British people “are the boss” when it comes to what the UK will accept.

Jonathan Reynolds said the focus for British negotiations was the EU summit in November to seal an agreement to ease some trading restrictions and a youth mobility deal. But he said the prime minister, Andy Burnham, had made it clear he wanted to see a long-term and stable relationship with the bloc.

“They will be talking to them about what the future options for our relationship might be. But here and now, the focus should be on the summit and the tangible difference improvements we’re going to make,” he told BBC Radio 4’s Today programme.

He said the changes announced at the EU summit, including a food and drink deal and on emissions trading, would make a significant difference. But he added: “What the prime minister has also said, quite rightly, is we face a series of policy changes on the European side that will affect us, and we need to get as little uncertainty as possible.

“We need to have a way to resolve what our long-term relationship will be, and he is going to set that out after the summit. And he is going to be frank with the British people about what the trade-offs are.”

Reynolds said there had been “no assumptions made about the next stage after that. It’s the prime minister quite rightly saying he wants to set out to the British people what the options are to resolve some of the tensions that exist.”

He said no decision would be made without voters being given a say, without specifying whether this would be through a general election or a referendum. “No assumptions have been made on this. The British people are the people who will decide this. They’re the boss on this,” he said.

A number of backbenchers with heavily pro-Brexit seats in the north and Midlands are wary about reopening the arguments for free movement or rejoining the EU.

The Labour MP for Great Grimsby and Cleethorpes, Melanie Onn, was among those urging caution. “I am still not over the kind of very divisive debate that we had when we were leaving the EU post the referendum, and I don’t think that the scars have healed for many people around the country,” she told Today. “So I do think that this is an unnecessary conversation to be having at this time.”

The Reform UK leader, Nigel Farage, told the programme it would be “very, very poor indeed” to begin discussing freedom of movement. “Five months ago, Andy Burnham says to leave-voting Makerfield: ‘I’m not going to reopen these arguments.’ Two and a half years ago, a Labour government got a massive majority in parliament saying we will not be re-entering the single market, the customs union, or opening up the debate on the free movement of people,” he said.

“What I am saying is this: this prime minister has no mandate, and to hear that his officials are negotiating in Brussels already is really very, very poor indeed.”

Burnham made his plea for an “honest discussion” about freedom of movement in an interview with the Sunday Times, making clear that staying out of it would no longer be a red line in talks with the EU.

Burnham also shared his belief that rejoining the single market and accepting free movement could be done without the need for another referendum, if Labour put it in its manifesto. In contrast, he said fully rejoining would need another in-out poll.

The prime minister said: “Freedom of movement had very significant challenges associated with it and I spoke about them in my previous time in parliament. But, at the same time, it was a type of immigration that perhaps wasn’t as difficult [as now].

“Revolving-door migration where people would come and then go back is different. The balance of migration has changed, hasn’t it, since then? I think we’ve just got to have an honest discussion about those things.”

#225. 'I would do it again' - Kaepernick reflects on price of protest, 10 years on

'I would do it again' - Kaepernick reflects on price of protest, 10 years on

"I knew the protest could potentially cost me my career...I would absolutely do it again."

Ten years on from making the gesture that catapulted him to iconic status, but which he believes led to his exile from the sport he loved, Colin Kaepernick insists he has no regrets.

It was September 2016 when NFL star Kaepernick knelt during the US national anthem before a match as a peaceful protest against racial injustice and police brutality.

It proved both inspirational and divisive.

In his first interview with BBC Sport, Kaepernick reflects on his journey; his pride at seeing England footballers take the knee, athlete activism, the relationship between sport and politics, and the price he paid for being a figurehead for the civil rights movement.

The former quarterback says he decided to protest despite suspecting it could risk his career.

"History shows usâ¦that is a reality for athletes", he says.

"We also see this in broader movements, going well beyond costing someone their career, and costing people their lives. We see Malcolm X assassinated, we see MLK [Martin Luther King] assassinated, so the consequences are real."

Having initially sat to raise awareness of the plight of black people in the US, Kaepernick first knelt during the Star-Spangled Banner before his San Francisco 49ers team played a pre-season game. It was an act he would repeat before 11 regular-season contests that year.

At the time, he said he could not stand up for - and show pride in - a flag "for a country that oppresses black people and people of colour", adding: "This is bigger than football and it would be selfish on my part to look the other way."

His stance against racial inequality was seen as heroic by many, inspiring other NFL players to follow suit. But there was also condemnation from those who said it was disrespectful to the military.

Those dissenting voices included Donald Trump - then a presidential candidate.

Trump described the act as "a terrible thing" and unpatriotic. He even suggested Kaepernick should find another country to live in, adding players who refused to stand for the national anthem should be fired.

The NFL said it would fine teams whose players did not stand.

Kaepernick opted out of his contract with the 49ers in March 2017, though the club's management later said he would have been released regardless.

Despite leading the 49ers to the 2013 Super Bowl, and throwing 16 touchdowns in his final season, one of the most talented quarterbacks in the sport never found a new team.

Speaking from New York, Kaepernick says he anticipated the reaction to taking the knee. "What I couldn't have predicted was the people who would show up for me in significant ways, and also the people who worked against me and against the movement," he adds.

Kaepernick eventually filed a grievance against NFL team owners, who he believed had conspired not to hire him because of his protests, reaching a financial settlement in 2019.

That year, the NFL arranged a private workout for Kaepernick, but he chose not to attend, instead holding his own session elsewhere to allow media to be present. Hopes of a comeback never materialised.

The NFL later admitted it was "wrong for not listening to players earlier", and encouraged "all to speak out and peacefully protest".

Colin Kaepernick speaks to BBC's Dan Roan

Former NFL quarterback Colin Kaepernick gives his first interview with BBC Sport, 10 years on from making the gesture that catapulted him to iconic status

Kaepernick first took the knee in protest in September 2016

Following the murder of George Floyd while in police custody in Minneapolis in 2020, taking the knee became a feature of Black Lives Matter protests, and widespread across sport.

England's men's and women's football teams were among those to follow suit.

"That was a surprising moment for me," says Kaepernick.

"I did think about the protest going beyond the US and how that may show up in other countries. To be able to see the national teams going out and protesting, it was humbling. I was happy that they were doing it.

"I also know there's still a lot of work to be done within racism in the UK and more broadly. And this is a global challenge that we are collectively fighting against.

"I was very excited to be able to see players actively calling that out, and willing to begin taking action towards addressing it."

Despite keeping a relatively low profile for most of the past decade, Kaepernick remains a standard-bearer for the civil rights movement.

But while surveys suggest the vast majority of professional athletes intend to use their social media channels to raise awareness about racial injustice, external, he accepts the momentum behind athlete activism seems to have slowed in recent years.

It was notable that several players across five clubs chose to remain standing when Premier League players took the knee as part of October's No Room for Racism campaign.

Anti-discrimination campaign group Kick It Out told BBC Sport the gesture "still has value in raising awareness of the structural inequalities that exist in football and society", but added it could "understand the reasons players might become frustrated with the pace of change".

Reports of discrimination received by the charity more than doubled in the past five years, reaching record levels last season.

"I think we're in a moment that demands protests more than ever," says Kaepernick.

"Why I think protests are not as loud right now [is] because of the concerted efforts that have been taken by organisations like the NFL, like the Trump administration, and corporate entities following along and capitulating in that process.

"While those things are happening, I think the reality of the state of the US, and more broadly the world, demands that we show up in a more significant way."

Kaepernick is all too familiar with how polarising it can be to take a political stand in sport.

Opponents of taking the knee have claimed it is a political and divisive statement, and that rather than demonstrating backing for the Black Lives Matter movement, it has shown support for the organisation of the same name.

In 2018, critics encouraged people to destroy Nike goods after the sportswear giant used Kaepernick as the face of an advertising campaign featuring the caption 'Believe in something. Even if it means sacrificing everything.'

So what does he say to those who argue that sport and politics should be kept apart?

"They ring hollow and feel very hypocritical, because politics is inherently part of sports," he says.

"We see within [American] football, we see taxpayers funding NFL stadiums. We see our NFL owners going out and supporting political candidates... so to try to position this as if sports aren't political, in many ways feels just laughable to me, and disconnected.

"Now, where the question comes in for people is: 'We don't want the athletes to be political. Everything around them can be political, but the athletes themselves, we don't want them to have a voice in the politics, because that interrupts our entertainment and our ability to disconnect.' For me, that's where society has a challenge with it."

Having encountered significant opposition from high-profile athletes during his first term, Trump seems to have encountered less challenge from the world of sport during his second, notably becoming the first sitting US president to attend the Super Bowl.

He has also appeared at golf's Ryder Cup, and the finals of football's Club World Cup and World Cup, though he was also booed when attending the NBA Finals in New York in June.

"It ties into the conversation of 'are sports political?' when he is going out and actively trying to use them to gain favour and popularity", says Kaepernick, adding that Fifa's controversial decision to award the US President its inaugural 'Peace Prize' last year was "regrettable".

"The part that I think is great to see is when he's showing up to these events and getting booed by the crowd, which speaks to the political environment that he's created, that the majority of the country does not agree withâ¦when it comes to sports, people need to continue to respond accordingly."

Despite backlash, Kaepernick repeated taking the knee before 11 regular-season NFL contests

Now 38, Kaepernick has published a memoir titled The Perilous Fight, detailing his childhood in California having been adopted by a white family, encountering racism, and his education and evolution. "People saw the moment. But they didn't see the years that made it possible", he says. "The questions about who I was; the injustices I could no longer ignore; the voices of those who came before me that I carried into that stadium...When I took a knee, it wasn't a sudden act. It was the result of years of becoming."

So how did he find the process of reliving that journey?

"Are there moments that are challenging going back to? Yes. But I hope that insight helps other people navigate situations...that helps them move forward or helps them to go accomplish things."

Kaepernick also supports community organisations via his Know Your Rights education campaign, is a philanthropist, and has written a number of children's books.

Even now, he maintains the fitness regime he had during his time at the 49ers, just in case an unlikely opportunity to resume his NFL career arises.

Kaepernick may not have thrown a ball in anger for almost a decade, but he is widely regarded as one of the most significant athletes in modern history.

The way he used his platform to promote change has been compared to legendary boxer Muhammad Ali's support of the anti-war movement, and US athletes Tommie Smith and John Carlos giving the Black Power Salute at the 1968 Olympics.

"To even be in the same conversation with the likes of [them], is extremely humbling," he says. "But more importantly, I just try to stay focused on the work.

"What can we do to help people? And how do we collectively move forward as a society? And, ultimately, how we build a society that works for everybody. That feels like a very simple principle that has been lost.

"I know in the US specifically, we have a lot of work to do there. I hope we begin taking the steps to get back to a place where we are actually addressing people's needs. And create a society that is equitable for everyone."

#223. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#221. Physicists trap a single antimatter particle for a month

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Scientists held a single positron, the antimatter counterpart of an electron, in a trap for weeks and used it to probe quantum behavior with unusual precision. The work could help test fundamental symmetries in physics and sharpen searches for differences between matter and antimatter.
